\mu(\{x\in A_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}(f)(x)-a_{n}|\geqslant t\})\leqslant Ce^{-bt}\text{ for all }t\geqslant 0\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then the flow
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
has singular spectrum and is spectrally disjoint from all mixing flows.

Both conclusions of this criterion were already proved in
[
CFKU
]
, see
[
CFKU
, Theorem 3.1]
, for the
spectral singularity, and
[
CFKU
, Remark 3.2]
, for the spectral disjointness to mixing flows.
The proof given in
[
CFKU
]
in turn uses as a tool a result from
[
FrLe05
]
, which is a version of Prokhorov weak compactness of tight sequences along rigidity sets.
We remark that
the
exponential tails
assumption, i.e. (
7
), along rigidity sets implies in particular that the sequence of centred Birkhoff sums
(S_{h_{n}}(f)(x)-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
tight
.

4.2.
Spectral disjointness criterion for special flows

We now state the spectral disjointness criterion that we will use to prove spectral disjointness: this follows from the existence
of a sequence of times where one flow is
rigid with exponential tails
and the other
mixing
.
Mixing
along a subsequence
is defined as follows:

Definition 4.2

(mixing sequence)
.

A measure-preserving flow
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
(Y,\nu)
is
mixing along a sequence

(a_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
of real numbers converging to
+\infty
if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle U_{a_{n}}g_{1},g_{2}\rangle=\langle g_{1},1\rangle\langle 1,g_{2}\rangle\quad\text{ for all }\quad g_{1},g_{2}\in{L^{2}}(Y,\nu),

Equivalently, the sequence of Koopman operators associated to
U_{a_{n}}

converges to the zero operator in the weak operator topology on
L^{2}_{0}(Y,\nu)
.
The criterion for spectral disjointness that we use is the following.

Theorem 7
.

Assume that
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
(X^{f},\mu^{f})
is a special flow as in the assumptions of Theorem
6
,
such that there exist
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Borel sets with
\mu(A_{n})\to 1
as
n\to+\infty
,
and
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
(S_{h_{n}}(f)(x)-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has exponential tails.

If
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is a measure-preserving flow on
(Y,\nu)
which is mixing along the sequence
(a_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
, then the flows
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are spectrally disjoint.

The rest of this section is dedicated to the proof of this criterion.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

In the proof, we need the notion of
integral operator
: for every probability Borel measure
P
on
\mathbb{R}
denote by
\int_{\mathbb{R}}T^{f}_{t}\,dP(t):L^{2}(X^{f},\mu^{f})\to L^{2}(X^{f},\mu^{f})

the operator such that

\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}T^{f}_{t}\,dP(t)(h_{1}),h_{2}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle T^{f}_{t}(h_{1}),h_{2}\rangle\,dP(t)\quad\text{ for all }\quad h_{1},h_{2}\in L^{2}(X^{f},\mu^{f}).

From the assumption, we know that the sequence of random variables
S_{h_{n}}(f)-a_{n}:A_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
n\geqslant 1
is
tight
and has exponential tails. This, by classical weak-compactness arguments, implies (as shown for example in one of the steps of the proof of Theorem 3.1 in
[
CFKU
]
)
that there exists a probability Borel measure
P
(obtained as a weak limit distribution)
such that

(8)

P((-\infty,-t)\cup(t,+\infty))\leqslant Ce^{-bt}\quad\text{for all}\quad t\geqslant 0.

(i.e. the limit probability also has exponential tails) and, by Theorem 6 in
[
FrLe05
]
, we have

(9)

T^{f}_{a_{n}}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}T^{f}_{-t}\,dP(t)\quad\text{in the weak operator topology on}\quad L^{2}(X^{f},\mu^{f}).

Suppose, contrary to the claimed conclusion, that the flow
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
(Y,\nu)
, which is mixing along
(a_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
, is not spectrally disjoint from
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
. Then there exist non-zero
g_{1}\in L^{2}_{0}(X^{f},\mu^{f})
and
g_{2}\in L^{2}_{0}(Y,\nu)
such that their spectral measures are equal, i.e.
\sigma^{T^{f}}_{g_{1}}=\sigma^{U}_{g_{2}}=\sigma\neq 0
. By assumption,
U_{a_{n}}\to 0
in the weak operator topology on
L_{0}^{2}(Y,\nu)
. It follows that the unitary representation
(V_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma)
, defined by (
2
), satisfies

(10)

V_{a_{n}}\to 0\quad\text{in the weak operator topology on}\quad L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma).

Hence
\sigma
is a continuous measure. Indeed, suppose, contrary to our claim, that
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is an atom of
\sigma
. Let
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma)
be the indicator function of the singleton
s_{0}
. As
V_{t}h(s)=e^{its}h(s)
, we have
\langle V_{t}h,h\rangle=e^{is_{0}t}\sigma(\{s_{0}\})
,
so
|\langle V_{a_{n}}h,h\rangle|=\sigma(\{s_{0}\})
for all
n\geqslant 1
contrary to (
10
).
By (
9
), we also have

V_{a_{n}}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{-t}\,dP(t)\quad\text{in the weak operator topology on}\quad L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma).

Together with (
10
), this gives
\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{-t}\,dP(t)=0
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma)
. Therefore for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\sigma)
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{-t}\,dP(t)(h_{1}),h_{2}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-its}h_{1}(s)\overline{h_{2}}(s)\,d\sigma(s)\,dP(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{P}(s)h_{1}(s)\overline{h_{2}}(s)\,d\sigma(s),

where
\widehat{P}
is the Fourier transform of the measure
P
. Therefore (since
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are arbitrary),
\widehat{P}(s)=0
for
\sigma
a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}
. As
\sigma
is continuous, we have
\widehat{P}=0
on an uncountable subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
On the other hand, as
P
has exponentially decaying tails (see (
8
)), its Fourier transform
\widehat{P}
is an analytic function on
\mathbb{R}
.
It follows that
\widehat{P}\equiv 0
, contrary to non-triviality of the measure
P
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Singularity from trimmed Birkhoff sums

In this section we reduce the verification of the criterion for spectral singularity (Theorem
6
) for the class of special flows we are interested in, to a result about Birkhoff sums of the
derivative

f^{\prime}
of the roof function
f
. Both the tightness and exponential tails required for the spectral singularity criterion will be proven through these bounds. We first introduce the notion of
trimmed Birkhoff sum
, state the main result on trimmed Birkhoff sums (Proposition
5.6
) which will be proved in §
8
, and then deduce from it the proof of singularity (namely of Theorem
4
).

5.1.
Birkhoff sums of the derivative trimming

Since we assume that
f
has logarithmic singularities, the derivative
f^{\prime}
of
f
will have
power-like singularities
of type
1/x
.

5.1.1.
Form of the singularities

To describe these singularities, analogously to what we did for the logarithmic singularities of
f
,
let us use the following auxiliary function
u
:

(11)

u(x):=-\frac{1}{(x)^{pos}},\qquad\text{where}\ (x)^{pos}:=\begin{cases}x&\text{if}\ 0<x\leqslant 1,\\
+\infty&\text{if}\ x\leqslant 0.\end{cases}

(here
(x)^{pos}
denotes, in a sense, the positive part of
x
). Recalling the definition of
\mathrm{Log}
in (
4
) one can check that the definition is given so that
u(x)=\mathrm{Log}^{\prime}(x)
.
Let us then consider
u_{i}^{+}(x):=u(x-\beta_{i})
, for
i=0,\dots,d-1
and
u_{i}^{-}(x):=-u(\beta_{i}-x)
,
i=1,\dots,d
. Then one sees that if
f
has pure logarithmic singularities of the form (
5
), then the derivative
f^{\prime}
has the form:

(12)

f^{\prime}(x)=-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\frac{C_{i}^{+}}{(x-\beta_{i})^{pos}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{C_{i}^{-}}{(\beta_{i}-x)^{pos}}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}u_{i}^{+}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}u^{-}_{i}(x).

We remark that this function is not in
L^{1}
(since singularities of type
1/x
are
not
integrable). To bound the derivative Birkhoff sums
S_{n}f^{\prime}
it is necessary to consider separately the contributions of the largest terms, which are controlled by the closest visits of an orbit to the singularities. We therefore introduce the notation for closest visits to singularities and define
trimmed
Birkhoff sums.

5.1.2.
Closest visits

Given an IET
T:I\to I
, a point
x\in I
and a natural number
r
, let us consider the orbit segment of length
r
,

\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r):=\{x,T(x),\dots,T^{r-1}(x)\}.

For every discontinuity
\beta_{i}
with
0\leqslant i<d
, denote by
x_{i}^{+}=x_{i}^{+}(x,r)
the closest visits of the orbit segment to
\beta_{i}

from the right
, and, correspondingly, by

m_{i}^{+}=m_{i}^{+}(x,r)
the minimum distance of the orbit points to
\beta_{i}

from the right
, namely:

m_{i}^{+}=m_{i}^{+}(x,r):=\min\{(T^{j}x-{\beta}_{i})^{pos},\\
0\leqslant j<r\},\quad{x_{i}^{+}=x_{i}^{+}(x,r):={\beta}_{i}+m_{i}^{+}},\ \ i=0,\dots,d-1,

where
(x)^{pos}
the ’positive’ part
of
x
defined in (
11
). Similarly, denote by
x_{i}^{-}:=x_{i}^{-}(x,r)
, for
i=1,\dots,d
, the closest visit to
{\beta}_{i}
among the points of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)

from the left
, and correspondingly by
m_{i}^{-}:=m_{i}^{-}(x,r)
, for
i=1,\dots,d
, the minimum distance from the
{\beta}_{i}
of the points of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
from the left
31
31

31

We exclude now
i=0
since
\beta_{0}=0
cannot be approached from the left.
:

m_{i}^{-}=m_{i}^{-}(x,r):=\min\{({\beta}_{i}-T^{j}x)^{pos},\ 0\leqslant j<r\},\quad{x_{i}^{-}=x_{i}^{-}(x,r):=\beta_{i}-m_{i}^{-}(x,r),}\ \ i=1,\dots,d.

Moreover, denote by
m(x,r)
the
minimum distance to the singularity set
of
T
given by

\displaystyle m(x,r):=

\displaystyle\min\big\{\{m_{i}^{+}(x,r),\ 0\leqslant i<d\}\cup\{m_{j}^{-}(x,r),\ \ 1\leqslant j\leqslant d\}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\left\{\left|T^{k}(x)-\beta_{i}\right|,\ \ 0\leqslant k<r,\ 0\leqslant i\leqslant d\right\}.

5.1.3.
Definition of trimmed Birkhoff sums

We can now introduce the notion of
trimmed
Birkhoff sums. Since we will use it both for the derivative
f^{\prime}
of the form (
12
) and for the function
f
, let us define it more generally for functions
v:\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}I_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
of the form
v=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}v^{+}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}v_{i}^{-}
,
where the functions
v_{i}^{+}
are monotonically decreasing, have a right-singularity at
\beta_{i}
(i.e. blow up as
x\to\beta_{i}^{+}
) and
v_{i}^{-}
are monotonically increasing and have a left singularity at
\beta_{i}
(i.e. blow up as
x\to\beta_{i}^{-}
). (Then both
f
and
f^{\prime}
have this form when taking
v_{i}^{\pm}:=\mathrm{Log}_{i}^{\pm}
for
f
and, respectively,
v_{i}^{\pm}:=u_{i}^{\pm}
for
f^{\prime}
).
A trimmed Birkhoff sum is then defined removing from Birkhoff sums all contributions given by all these closest visits to singularities, from the right and from the left:

Definition 5.1
.

Given an IET
T
and a function
v
as above, the
trimmed Birkhoff sum

\widetilde{S}_{r}v
is defined for every
x\in I\setminus End(T)
as

\widetilde{S}_{r}v(x):=S_{r}v(x)-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C^{+}_{i}v_{i}^{+}(x_{i}^{+}(x,r))-\sum_{i=1}^{d}C^{-}_{i}v_{i}^{-}(x_{i}^{-}(x,r)).

Notice that, since the functions
v_{i}^{\pm}
are monotonic,
|v_{i}^{\pm}(x_{i}^{\pm}(x,r))|

are the largest values of
|v_{i}^{\pm}|
evaluated along the orbit
segment
\mathcal{O}(x,r)
; this justifies the use of the terminology
trimmed
Birkhoff sums (
trimming
a Birkhoff sums usually refers to removing the largest element from the sum, see e.g.
[
DiVa
,
KeSc
,
AuSc
]
).

This definition applies in particular to
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
with
pure
logarithmic singularities.
Let us extend this definition to
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
, by writing
f=f^{\operatorname{p}}+g
, where
f^{\operatorname{p}}\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
and
g
is piecewise absolutely continuous and setting

(13)

\widetilde{S}_{n}f:=\widetilde{S}_{n}f^{\operatorname{p}}+S_{n}g.

5.2.
Bounds of trimmed derivatives along rigidity towers

We now state the form of control of trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivatives of the roof which we will prove and will allow us to verify the assumptions of the spectral singularity criterion.
The estimates that we need give a
linear
control of the trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative over a suitable large set which is a rigidity set. Such rigidity set for the IET on the base will be given by large Rohlin towers (namely
rigidity towers
, that we now define).

5.2.1.
Rigidity towers

The rigidity sets that we will build for the base IETs will all be given by (Rohlin) towers by intervals:

Definition 5.2

(towers by intervals)
.

Given an interval
J:=(a,b)\subset I:=[0,1]
and
h\in\mathbb{N}
we say that the union

\mathcal{T}:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{h-1}T^{i}J

is a (Rohlin)
tower by intervals
of
base

J
and of
height

h
for the IET
T:I\to I
if and only if the images
T^{i}J,0\leqslant i<h
are pairwise disjoint intervals which do not contain any discontinuity for
T
.

Notice that since
T
is a piecewise isometry and the floors of a tower are disjoint, the (Lebesgue)
measure

|\mathcal{T}|
of the Rohlin tower is

(14)

0\leqslant|\mathcal{T}|=h|J|=h|b-a|\leqslant 1.

We recall Rohlin towers by intervals for IETs can be produced naturally by
inducing
: if
J\subset[0,1]
is a subinterval and
T
a minimal IET,
the first return map of
T
to
I
is an interval exchange of at most
d+2
exchanged intervals, and the return time on each of them is constant. If
h_{j}
is the return time on the continuity interval
J_{i}\subset J
, then
\mathcal{T}^{J}_{i}:=\cup_{k=0}^{h_{j}-1}T^{k}(J_{i})
is a Rohlin tower by intervals, that we will call
tower over the inducing interval

J_{i}
.

Definition 5.3

(rigidity towers)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, an
\epsilon
-
rigidity tower
for the IET
T:I\to I
is a
tower by intervals
of
base

J
of
height

h
for the IET
T:I\to I

if we have that

d(T^{h}x,x)<{\epsilon}|J|\leqslant\frac{\epsilon}{h},\qquad\text{for\ every}\ x\in J,

(the second inequality being automatic since
|J|h\leqslant 1
).

Furthermore, we say that a sequence
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of towers by intervals is a sequence of
rigidity towers
for the IET
T
if there exists a sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that each
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is
\epsilon_{n}
-rigid and
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

5.2.2.
Linearly bounded trimmed derivatives

The form of control on Birkhoff sums of derivatives that we need is the following, which shows that, at special times and for sums inside a Rohlin tower,
\widetilde{S}_{r}v(x)
grows
linearly
(as expected for an integrable function, although
v
is
not
integrable). We stress that here the
symmetry
of the singularities plays a crucial role, since if the singularities are logarithmic but
asymmetric
, the growth is known to be faster than linear
32
32

32

If
v=1/x
, one can show that for a.e. IET
T
the Birkhoff sums
S_{r}v
grown like
r\log r
, i.e. that
S_{r}v/r\log r
converges in probability to
1
(as random variable in
x
). In
[
AuSc
]
, when
T
is a rotation, it shown that
S_{r}v(x)/r\log r
converges for almost every
x\in[0,1]
.
, as shown in
[
Ulc07
,
Rav17
]
.

Definition 5.4

(linearly bounded trimmed derivatives)
.

Given an IET
T
, a function
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
and a sequence
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Rohlin towers by intervals with bases
J_{n}\subset[0,1]
and heights
h_{n}
for
T
, we say that
f
has
linearly bounded trimmed derivatives
along the Rohlin towers
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(for
T
) if there exists a constant
M>0
such that
the trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative satisfy

\left|\widetilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(x)\right|\leqslant Mh_{n}\ \ \text{for \ any}\ x\in J_{n}\text{\ and\ any}\ 0\leqslant r\leqslant h_{n}.

In this case, to specify the constant
M>0
, we say that the sequence
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has
M
-bounded trimmed derivatives.

Thus, the linearly bounded trimmed derivatives assumption provides uniform control for all trimmed Birkhoff sums (using the extended definition (
13
) of trimming) of points from the base of any tower up to the height.

5.2.3.
Trimmed derivative bounds along balanced Rohlin towers

Linear bounds on trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative
S_{n}f^{\prime}
of a function with symmetric logarithmic singularities were proved by the last author in
[
Ulc11
]
, (since they are at the core of the result on absence of mixing for locally Hamiltonian flows proved in
[
Ulc11
]
). With the notation for trimmed Birkhoff sums we just introduced in §
5.1.3
, Proposition 4.2 in
[
Ulc11
]
can indeed be restated as follows:

Proposition 5.5

(Prop. 4.2 in
[
Ulc11
]
)
.

For a.e. IET
T
, there exists a sequence of nested intervals
(I^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
such that every
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
has linearly bounded trimmed derivative Birkhoff along the towers
(\mathcal{T}^{\ell}_{j})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
over the inducting intervals
I^{\ell}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, i.e. there exists a constant

M>0
such that:

(15)

\left|\widetilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(x)\right|\leqslant Mh^{\ell}_{j},\quad\text{for \ all}\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant d,\ x\in I^{\ell}_{j},\ 0\leqslant r\leqslant h^{\ell}_{j},

where
h^{\ell}_{j}
is the first return time of
I^{\ell}_{j}
to
I^{\ell}
, i.e. the height of the tower
\mathcal{T}^{\ell}_{j}
.

We will not use
Proposition
5.5
, directly,
but we will exploit it indirectly to prove singularity, see §
8
, as we explain in the next §
5.2.4
.

5.2.4.
Trimmed derivative bounds along rigidity towers

The main technical result that we need to prove singularity is the following.

Proposition 5.6

(derivative bounds along rigid towers)
.

For a.e. IET
T
, there exists a sequence of rigidity towers
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
with
|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|\to 1
such that every
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)

has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives along the sequence
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Section
8
is devoted to the proof of this Proposition.
We stress that the towers provided by this Proposition
5.6
are
rigidity sets
. Linear bounds on trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative also
along the towers produced by Proposition
5.5
, but, by nature of their construction, those towers are
balanced
33
33

33

The balance of the towers
\mathcal{T}^{\ell}_{j}

produced in the proof of Proposition
5.5

in
[
Ulc11
]
plays indeed an essentially role in the proof of the cancellations in the Birkhoff sums of the derivative (which are very delicate and depend on a sophisticated Diophantine-type condition for the IET, see §
C
or
[
Ulc11
]
for more details).
(i.e. have measure uniformly bounded away from
0
and
1
, see Remark
8.2
).
In order to apply the singularity criterion given by Proposition
6
, on the other hand, we require that linear bounds hold along towers which are
rigidity towers
(i.e. have measure tending to
1
).

5.3.
Proof of singularity from trimmed bounds along rigidity towers

In this section, assuming the Proposition
5.6
just stated, we conclude the proof of singularity (Theorem
4
). We want in particular to verify the assumptions of the spectral singularity criterion (Theorem
6
), namely
tightness
and
exponential tails
along a rigidity set.
We first recall how to obtain rigidity sets from rigid towers for IETs (see §
5.3.1
). The heart of the verification, namely that linear bounds from trimmed derivatives along rigid towers allow to prove the exponential tails, is proved in §
5.3.2
, the final arguments are then summarized in §
5.3.3
.

5.3.1.
Rigidity sets in rigid towers.

The existence of a rigidity tower in an IET immediately gives a rigidity set.
We include the short proof of this well known mechanism for rigidity in IETs for convenience for the reader (highlightening the description of the dynamics inside the tower, which will be useful for later proofs).

Lemma 5.7

(almost cylinders inside rigidity towers)
.

If
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is a sequence of rigidity towers for an IET
T
on
[0,1]
such that
|\mathcal{T}_{n}|\to 1
as
n\to\infty
, the sequence

(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of their heights is a rigidity sequence for
T
. Furthermore, if
J_{n}
is the base of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, then there exists a sequence of smaller rigidity towers
C_{n}\subset\mathcal{T}_{n}
of base
J^{\prime}_{n}\subset J_{n}
, still satisfying
|C_{n}|\to 1
, such that
T^{h_{n}}J^{\prime}_{n}\subset J_{n}
and
T^{h_{n}}
acts as an isometry on every floor of
C_{n}
.

Definition 5.8

(almost cylinders)
.

We will call the sets
(C_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given by this Lemma the
almost cylinders
given by the rigidity towers
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Since by assumption each floor
T^{i}J_{n}
,
0\leqslant i<h_{n}
of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is an interval of
continuity
for
T
,
T^{i}
acts isometrically on
J_{n}
for every
0\leqslant i\leqslant h_{n}
. By assumption there exists a sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
such that
d(T^{h_{n}}z,z)\leqslant\delta_{n}
for every
z\in J_{n}
, where
\delta_{n}:=\epsilon_{n}|b_{n}-a_{n}|
. Then, we can find a smaller subinterval
J_{n}^{\prime}\subset J_{n}
such that
T^{h_{n}}(J_{n}^{\prime})\subset J_{n}
and
|J_{n}^{\prime}|=|J_{n}|-\epsilon_{n}
, by taking
J_{n}^{\prime}=(a_{n}+\delta_{n},b_{n})
if
T^{h_{n}}a_{n}\leqslant a_{n}
or
(a_{n},b_{n}-\delta_{n})
otherwise. Then setting
C_{n}
to be the Rohlin tower of height
h_{n}
over
J_{n}^{\prime}
, we have that
34
34

34

To see this formally, given
x\in C_{n}
, write
x=T^{k}x_{0}
for some
x_{0}\in J_{n}^{\prime}
and
0\leqslant k<h_{n}
. Then
T^{h_{n}}(x)=T^{k}T^{h_{n}-k}(x)

T^{h_{n}}(x)\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
for every
x\in C_{n}
and that
|C_{n}|=|J_{n}^{\prime}|h_{n}=(1-\epsilon_{n})|J_{n}|h_{n}\to 1
since
|\mathcal{T}_{n}|=|J_{n}|h_{n}\to 1
.
∎

5.3.2.
Exponential tails from trimmed derivative bounds

We now show that this control on large Rohlin towers filling the space suffices to show tightness and exponential bounds.

Proposition 5.9

(exponential tails via trimmed derivatives)
.

If
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of rigidity towers for
T
with

|\mathcal{T}_{n}|\to 1\ \text{as}\ n\to\infty

and
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
has
linearly bounded trimmed derivatives
along
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, then there exists centering constants
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

such that the sequence
(S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has exponential tails.

The rest of this subsubsection is devoted to the proof of this Proposition. In essence, the uniform bounds on the trimmed sums gives tightness, while the contribution of closest visits are responsible for the exponential tails of the distribution.

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

To prove that
S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}
have exponential tails we need to find sets
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
|A_{n}|\to 1
for which the exponential tail bound (
7
) holds on
A_{n}
. As sets
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
we will take the almost cylinders
(C_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given by the rigidity towers
\mathcal{T}_{n}
constructed in the proof of Lemma
5.7
, i.e. set
A_{n}:=C_{n}\subset\mathcal{T}_{n}
. We then want to prove tightness and exponential tails of
S_{h_{n}}f
on
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
after a suitable centering. For defining the
centering constants

(c_{n})
, let
z_{n}:=(b_{n}-a_{n})/2
be the midpoint of the base interval
J_{n}=(a_{n},b_{n})
(of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{n}
) and set

c_{n}:=S_{h_{n}}f(z_{n}),\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Given any
x\in C_{n}
, to study the difference
S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}
we therefore want to compare

S_{h_{n}}f(x)
and
S_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})
. A standard way to do so in a tower is
to write
x=T^{k}x_{n}
, where
x_{n}\in J^{\prime}_{n}
is a point in the base of
C_{n}
and
0\leqslant k<h_{n}
the height of the floor of the tower
C_{n}
which contains
x
. Then, using the cocycle properties of Birkhoff sums, we can add and subtract
S_{k}f(x_{n})
and split the Birkhoff sums difference
35
35

35

To do so, using the cocycle properties we see that
S_{h_{n}}f(x)=S_{h_{n}-k}f(x)+S_{k}f(T^{h_{n}-k}x)
and
S_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})=S_{k}f(x_{n})+S_{h_{n}-k}f(T^{k}x_{n})
. After subtracting, we get the sum of
S_{h_{n}}f(x)-S_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})=S_{k}f(T^{h_{n}}x_{n})-S_{k}f(x_{n})

(where we used that
x=T^{k}(x_{n})
).

(16)

S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}=\left(S_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})-S_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})\right)-{\left(S_{k}f(T^{h_{n}}x_{n})-S_{k}f(z_{n})\right)+\left(S_{k}f(x_{n})-S_{k}f(z_{n})\right)}.

Notice that both terms are now differences of Birkhoff sums along orbits that travel together inside a Rohlin tower, so we can apply to each difference the mean value theorem. We want, though, to first to set aside the contributions given by the closest singularities (which will produce the exponential tails) and then apply mean value only to the corresponding

trimmed
sums (to which we can apply the trimmed derivative bounds). By the definition of trimmed sums (see Definition
5.1
) and recalling the notation introduced in §
5.1.2
, for all
y\in I
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle S_{r}f(y)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}_{r}f(y)+\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}\mathrm{Log}_{i}^{+}(x_{i}^{+}(y,r))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}\mathrm{Log}_{i}^{-}(x_{i}^{-}(y,r))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}_{r}f(y){-}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}\log m_{i}^{+}(y,r){-}\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}\log m_{i}^{-}(y,r).

Thus we can estimate the first difference of Birkhoff sums in (
16
) by:

(17)

\displaystyle\left|S_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})-\right.\left.S_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})\right|\

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})-\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}\log\frac{m_{i}^{+}(x_{n},h_{n})}{m_{i}^{+}(z_{n},h_{n})}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}\log\frac{m_{i}^{-}(x_{n},h_{n})}{m_{i}^{-}(z_{n},h_{n})}\right|.

Let us first estimate the difference of trimmed Birkhoff sums in (
17
) using the mean value theorem. Notice that for any
x
in
J_{n}
we can differentiate and get
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}x}}\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f(x)=\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f^{\prime}(x)
(since
T^{k}
acts as an isometry on
(a_{n},b_{n})
for all
0\leqslant k<h_{n}
). Thus, by mean value first and then the linearly bounded trimmed derivative assumption, for some
\xi
between
x_{n}
and
z_{n}

\big|\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})-\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})\big|=\big|\widetilde{S}_{h_{n}}f^{\prime}(\xi)\big|\cdot|x_{n}-z_{n}|\leqslant Mh_{n}|b_{n}-a_{n}|\leqslant M.

Let us now estimate the sum in the RHS of (
17
) by estimating the contribution coming from closest visits. We claim that for every
i
for which they are defined and every
0<l\leqslant h_{n}
,

(18)

\left|\log\frac{m_{i}^{\pm}(x_{n},l)}{m_{i}^{\pm}(z_{n},l)}\right|\leqslant\max\left\{\log 2,-\log\left({h_{n}m(x_{n},l)}\right)\right\},

where
m(x_{n},l)
is the distance of the closest visit to the singularity set, which by definition satisfies

m(x_{n},l)\leqslant m_{i}^{\pm}(x_{n},l)
(see §
5.1.2
).
For this, remark that
\mathcal{T}_{n}
does not contain any endpoint of
End(T)
in its interior, and
T^{j}(z_{n})
are all midpoints of the corresponding floor
T^{j}(J_{n})
for all
0\leqslant j<h_{n}
(since

z_{n}
is the midpoint of
J_{n}
and each of these
T^{j}
acts as an isometry on
J_{n}
). Thus, this gives (since
T^{j}z_{n}
are all at least
|b_{n}-a_{n}|/2
far from
End(T)
, and
T^{j}x_{n}
belongs to the same floor of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{n}
as
T^{j}z_{n}
) that

\frac{|b_{n}-a_{n}|}{2}\leqslant m_{i}^{\pm}(z_{n},l)\leqslant m_{i}^{\pm}(x_{n},l)+\frac{|b_{n}-a_{n}|}{2},\quad m_{i}^{\pm}(x_{n},l)\leqslant m_{i}^{\pm}(z_{n},l)+\frac{|b_{n}-a_{n}|}{2}.

Using these estimates and recalling that
|b_{n}-a_{n}|\leqslant 1/h_{n}
by (
14
), we can get an upper and lower bound for the ratios:

\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\frac{m_{i}^{\pm}(z_{n},l)}{m_{i}^{\pm}(x_{n},l)}\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{2h_{n}m(x_{n},l)}\leqslant\max\left\{2,\frac{1}{h_{n}m(x_{n},l)}\right\},

(the last inequality follows from
a+b\leqslant 2\max\{a,b\}
). These upper and lower bounds imply the bounds (
18
) for the absolute value of their logarithms.
Thus, using this and the difference of trimmed Birkhoff sums bounds to estimate (
17
),
we get

\displaystyle\left|S_{h_{n}}f(x_{n})-S_{h_{n}}f(z_{n})\right|\leqslant M+C\max\{\log 2,-\log h_{n}m(x_{n},h_{n})|\},

where
C:=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}
.
The same reasoning can be carried out also for the second difference in (
16
) (to which mean value and then the linearly bounded trimmed derivative assumption also applies, since
T^{h_{n}}x_{n}
and
x_{n}
also both belong to the base
J_{n}=(a_{n},b_{n})
and
0\leqslant k<h_{n}
). Thus, adding up both analogous estimates, we get

(19)

|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}|\leqslant{3M+3C}\max\left\{\log 2,-\log{h_{n}\,m(x_{n},h_{n})},-\log h_{n}\,{m(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k)}\right\}.

To conclude, we now want to use this estimate to show that the tails are exponential. Indeed, (
19
) shows that for any
\alpha>M^{\prime}:={3M+3C\log 2}
,

(20)

\min\{m(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k),m(x_{n},h_{n})\}\geqslant\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha):=\frac{e^{{-(\alpha-M^{\prime})}/{3C}}}{h_{n}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}|\leqslant\alpha.

If we denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
the
\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha)
-neighbourhood of the endpoints, i.e.

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}:=\{x\in[0,1]:\ d(x,End(T))<\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha)\}
,
we then claim that

(21)

\{x\in C_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}|\geqslant\alpha\}\subset\bigcup_{-h_{n}<i<h_{n}}T^{i}\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}.

Indeed, if
x\in C_{n}
is such that
|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}|\geqslant\alpha
, by (
20
) either
m(x_{n},h_{n})
or
m(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k)
are less than
\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha)
, i.e. either the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}(x_{n},h_{n})
or
\mathcal{O}(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k)
intersect
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
.
Recalling that
x_{n}
is the projection to the base of the tower
C_{n}
of
x
, i.e.
x=T^{k}x_{n}
, one can see that the following inclusion of orbits holds:

\mathcal{O}(x_{n},h_{n})\cup\mathcal{O}(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k)\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{-(h_{n}-1)}x_{n},2h_{n}-1)=\{T^{-(h_{n}-1)}x,\dots,T^{-1}x,x,Tx\dots T^{h_{n}-1}x\},

so if either the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}(x_{n},h_{n})
or
\mathcal{O}(T^{h_{n}}x_{n},k)
intersect
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
, there exists
-h_{n}<i<h_{n}
such that
T^{i}(x)\in\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}

and (
21
) follows. Then, recalling the definition of
\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha)
in (
20
), we get

\left|\{x\in C_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-c_{n}|\geqslant\alpha\}\right|\leqslant 2h_{n}|\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}|=4dh_{n}\varepsilon_{n}(\alpha)={4d}{e^{\frac{M^{\prime}-\alpha}{3C}}}=Ke^{-\alpha/{3C}}

where
K:=4de^{M^{\prime}/{3C}}
. This
shows that the tails are exponential and hence finishes the proof.
∎

5.3.3.
Concluding the proof of singularity

We finish this section summarizing why with the proof of the proposition we also conclude the proof of the singularity result for special flows, i.e. Theorem
4
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Let us verify the assumptions of the spectral singularity criterion (Theorem
6
),
in particular the exponential tails assumption.
By Proposition
5.6
, there exists a sequence of rigidity towers
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
|\mathcal{T}_{n}|\to 1
with
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as a rigidity sequence such that
f^{\operatorname{p}}
has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives along the sequence
(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

By Lemma
5.7
and Proposition
5.9
, there exists a sequence of trimmed towers
(C_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

with
|C_{n}|\to 1
and such that
T^{h_{n}}J^{\prime}_{n}\subset J_{n}
, where
J_{n}
is the base of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
J^{\prime}_{n}
is the base of
C_{n}
, and the sequence
(S_{h_{n}}f^{\operatorname{p}}(x)-c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has exponential tails with
c_{n}=S_{h_{n}}f^{\operatorname{p}}(z_{n})
, where
z_{n}
is the midpoint of
J_{n}
, i.e. there are positive constants
C,b
such that

Leb\left(\left\{x\in C_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}f^{\operatorname{p}}(x)-c_{n}|\geqslant t\right\}\right)\leqslant Ce^{-bt}.

As
g=g_{f}:I\to\mathbb{R}
is of bounded variation and
T^{h_{n}}J^{\prime}_{n}\subset J_{n}
, standard arguments first used for IETs by Katok in
[
Ka80
]
, show that

|S_{h_{n}}g(x)-v_{n}|\leqslant V\quad\text{for}\quad x\in C_{n},\quad\text{where}\quad v_{n}=S_{h_{n}}g(z_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad V=\textrm{Var}(g).

As
f=f^{\operatorname{p}}+g
, taking
A_{n}:=C_{n}
and
a_{n}:=c_{n}+v_{n}
, it follows that

Leb\left(\left\{x\in A_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}|\geqslant t\right\}\right)\leqslant Ce^{-b(t-V)},

so the sequence
(S_{h_{n}}f(x)-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has exponential tails.
Since by assumption
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a rigidity sequence and the previous equation gives the exponential tails assumption (
7
), the spectral singularity criterion given by Theorem
6
implies that the special flow
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
has singular spectrum and is spectrally disjoint from all mixing flows.
∎

6.
Resonant mixing times and spectral disjointness

In this section we present the main arguments towards the proof of the spectral disjointness result for special flows (i.e. Theorem
5
). In §
6.1
we state the two results (Proposition
6.3
and Proposition
6.4
) from which (together with the spectral disjointness criterion given by Theorem
7
) we can deduce the proof of Theorem
5
. We use them to prove pairwise spectral disjointness in §
6.2
.

The rest of this section is then devoted to proving one of these two results (Proposition
6.3
), namely to showing that certain
resonant rigid times
(see Definition
6.2
)
are
mixing times
. After having introduced several auxiliary results in the subsections §
6.3
to §
6.5
, the proof of this proposition is given in §
6.6
.
The other result (Proposition
6.4
),
that gives the existence of infinitely many such
mixing resonant times
within a given sequence, will be proved in Section
9
.

6.1.
Main Steps in the proof of spectral disjointness

We present here the main results to prove spectral disjointness, which will be used to verify the assumptions of the spectral disjointness criterion (Theorem
7
).
For almost every IET
T
, to prove singularity of the spectrum of
(T^{f}_{t})
, we have already shown in the previous section the existence of a sequence of centering constants
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the distribution of Birkhoff sums
(S_{h_{n}}f-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for
T
is tight and has exponential tails.

The assumptions of the spectral disjointness criterion hold if there exists such a centering sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which is also a
mixing
sequence for the second flow (in the sense of Definition
4.2
). The type of times along which we want to prove mixing (resonant rigid times) is defined in §
6.1.1
. We state the two auxiliary results in §
6.1.2
and §
6.1.3

(Proposition
6.3
and
Proposition
6.4
respectively), from which, in the next §
6.2
,
we deduce Theorem
5
.

6.1.1.
Resonant rigid times

The mixing times for the second flow that we will use are
resonant
rigid times for the IET in the base, i.e. are multiples
kq
of a rigid time
q
where
k
is an integer
and
q,2q,\dots,kq
are all rigid times. We formalize this idea through the following definition. Recall that the notion of an
\epsilon
-
rigid tower by intervals
was defined in Definition
5.3
.

Definition 6.1
.

Given an IET
U
and
0<\epsilon<1
, we say that a natural number
q
is an
\epsilon
-
rigidity time
for
U
if
q
is the height of an
\epsilon
-rigid tower
\mathcal{T}
such that the measure of
\mathcal{T}
is greater than
1-\epsilon
.

Definition 6.2

(resonant rigidity time)
.

Given an IET
U
, we say that
r>0
is a
(\epsilon,k)
-
resonant time
, or simply a
resonant time
, if we can write
r=kq
where:

(i)

\epsilon>0
and
k
is an integer such that
1\leqslant k\leqslant\frac{1}{\epsilon}
;

(ii)

q
is the height of an
\epsilon^{2}
-rigid tower by intervals
\mathcal{T}
for
U
;

(iii)

if
B
is the base of the tower
\mathcal{T}
, the measure
|B|q>1-\epsilon
.

Given sequences
\underline{\epsilon}:=(\epsilon_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\underline{k}:=(k_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, where

\epsilon_{m}>0
and
k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
\underline{r}:=(r_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
sequence
of
(\underline{\epsilon},\underline{k})
-
resonant times
if, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
r_{m}
is an

(\epsilon_{m},k_{m})
-resonant time.

Notice that we request that
\mathcal{T}
is
\epsilon^{2}
-rigid (rather than only
\epsilon
-rigid). Since
k\leqslant 1/\epsilon
, this guarantees that not only the height
q
of
\mathcal{T}
is an
\epsilon
-rigidity time, but also its multiples
q,2q,\dots,kq
are all
\epsilon
-rigidity times.

6.1.2.
Mixing of resonant rigid times

We will show that resonant rigidity times (under suitable conditions) give mixing times (and so do times which are close to them).
Recall that a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a mixing sequence for the flow
(U^{h}_{t})_{t}
if for all measurable
A,B

\lim_{n\to\infty}Leb^{h}(A\cap U^{h}_{t_{n}}B)=Leb^{h}(A)Leb^{h}(B).

Let us recall that we denote by
\mathcal{I}_{d}
the space of
d
-IETs on
[0,1]
and by
m
the natural Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{I}_{d}
(see §
2.1.2
).

Proposition 6.3
.

For every
d\geqslant 2
, there exists a full-measure set
\mathcal{F}_{d}\subset\mathcal{I}_{d}
of the set
\mathcal{I}_{d}
of
d
-IETs such that
if
T\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
and
(k_{m}q_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
(\underline{\epsilon},\underline{k})
-resonant times for
T
with

\log\log q_{m}\geqslant k_{m},\quad\text{for\ all}\ m\in\mathbb{N},

then, for every roof function
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}^{\textrm{2+}}\left(T\right)
(with
\int f(x)\,dx=1
), the sequence
(k_{m}q_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a mixing sequence for the flow
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.
Furthermore, if
(t_{m})_{m\geqslant 1}
is a sequence for which there exists
C>1
such that

t_{m}\in[k_{m}q_{m},Ck_{m}q_{m}],\qquad\text{for all}\quad m\geqslant 1,

then also
(t_{m})_{m\geqslant 1}
is a
mixing sequence
for the flow
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.

We will prove this Proposition later in this section, see §
6.6
.

6.1.3.
Prescribing resonant rigid times

This type of mixing times, which are based on (resonant) rigidity turns out to be
frequent
. In particular, it is possible to control where they occur, so that they can be chosen to coincide (infinitely often) with a given sequence which will be later taken to be the sequence of centralizing constants for the Birkhoff sums of the first flow.
This is the content of the second result needed for spectral disjointness:

Proposition 6.4

(control of rigid times)
.

For every
d\geqslant 2
, there exists a constant

C=C_{d}>1
such that the following holds.
Fix
0<\epsilon<1
and

-

an increasing sequence
\underline{s}:=(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of real positive numbers converging to
\infty
.

Consider the subset
\Omega:=\Omega\left(\underline{s},{\epsilon}\right)\subset\mathcal{I}_{d}

consisting of all
d
-IETs on
[0,1]
for which
there exists an increasing sequence
(q_{j})_{j\geqslant 1}
such that:

(
\Omega
1)

q_{j}
is a
\epsilon
-rigid time
for all
j\geqslant 1
,

(
\Omega
2)

for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

s_{n_{j}}\leqslant q_{j}<Cs_{n_{j}}.

Then the set
\Omega
has full measure in
\mathcal{I}_{d}
, i.e.
m(\mathcal{I}_{d}\setminus\Omega)=0
.

The proof of this proposition, which is heavily based on Rauzy-Veech induction properties (in particular on Kerckhoff’s Lemma) and close in spirit to some of the technical arguments used by Chaika in
[
Cha
]
to show that almost every pair of IETs is disjoint, is given in Section
9
.

6.2.
Final arguments in the proof of spectral disjointness

Let us now show that Propositions
6.3
and
6.4
together imply the main spectral disjointness result for special flows (namely Theorem
5
).

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Let
T
be in the
full measure sets of IETs for which the conclusion of Proposition
5.6
holds. Then there exists a sequence of rigidity towers
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
with
|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|\to 1
such that for every
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
the function
f^{\operatorname{p}}
has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives along
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
. By Proposition
5.9
and the proof of Theorem
4
, there exists a sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the sequence
(S_{h_{n}}f-a_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
is tight with exponential tails, where
(h_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
is the sequence of heights of the towers
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, i.e. there are two positive constants
C
,
b
such that

\lambda(t)=Leb\left(\left\{x\in\mathcal{T}_{n}:|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}|\geqslant t\right\}\right)\leqslant Ce^{-bt}.

Let
J_{n}
be the base interval of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Then

\int_{J_{n}}|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}|dx\leqslant t\lambda(t)+\int_{t}^{+\infty}\lambda(s)\,ds+t|J_{n}|\leqslant(Ct+C/b)e^{-bt}+t|J_{n}|

for any
t\geqslant 1
and
n\geqslant 1
. Taking first the limit as
n\to+\infty
, and then as
t\to+\infty
, we obtain that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{J_{n}}|S_{h_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}|dx=0.

As
|\mathcal{T}_{n}|=h_{n}|J_{n}|\to 1
and
\int_{I}f(x)\,dx=1
, it follows that

1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{h_{n}}=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}|J_{n}|=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\int_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}f(x)\,dx-a_{n}|J_{n}|)=0.

Fix any increasing sequence
\underline{k}=(k_{j})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of natural numbers and take the sequence
\underline{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
given by
\epsilon_{j}:=k_{j}^{-1}
, so that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j
grows. Consider now the sequences
\underline{s}^{j}=(s^{j}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by

s_{n}^{j}:=C_{d}^{-2}h_{n}\epsilon_{j}=C_{d}^{-2}h_{n}k_{j}^{-1},\qquad\forall\ n\in\mathbb{N},

where
C_{d}>1
is a constant given by Proposition 6.4.
Let
{S}
be an IET in the intersection of the full measure set of IETs
\mathcal{F}_{d}
(for which the conclusion of Proposition
6.3
holds) and
the conclusion of Proposition
6.4
holds for all pairs
(\underline{s}^{j},\epsilon^{2}_{j})
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e.
S\in\mathcal{D}_{T}:=\mathcal{F}\cap\bigcap_{j\geqslant 1}\Omega(\underline{s}^{j},\epsilon_{j}^{2})
.
By Proposition
6.4
, for any such
S
, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exist infinitely many
\epsilon^{2}_{j}
-rigidity times
q
for
S
such that

C_{d}^{-2}h_{n}k_{j}^{-1}=s^{j}_{n}\leqslant q\leqslant C_{d}s^{j}_{n}=C_{d}^{-1}h_{n}k_{j}^{-1}\quad\text{for some}\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

It follows that there exist increasing sequences of natural numbers
(n_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
q_{j}
is an
\epsilon^{2}_{j}
-rigidity time,
\log\log q_{j}\geqslant k_{j}
and

C_{d}k_{j}q_{j}\leqslant h_{n_{j}}\leqslant C_{d}^{2}k_{j}q_{j}.

Hence,
(k_{j}q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
(\underline{\epsilon},\underline{k})
-resonant times. Since
a_{n}/h_{n}\to 1
, we also have

k_{j}q_{j}\leqslant a_{n_{j}}\leqslant 2C_{d}^{2}k_{j}q_{j}

for all
j
large enough.

Then by Proposition
6.3
, the sequence
(a_{n_{j}})_{j}
(that is trapped between successive resonant rigid times with
k_{j}\to\infty
) is a mixing subsequence for
(S_{t}^{g})
for any roof function
g\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}^{\textrm{2+}}\left(S\right)
.
Furthermore, (by the choice of
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at the beginning of the proof) we also have that
(S_{h_{n_{j}}}f-a_{n_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight with exponential tails. Thus, the assumptions of disjointness criterion (Theorem
7
) hold and the criterion implies that the special flows
T^{f}_{t}
and
S^{g}_{t}
are spectrally disjoint.
∎

The rest of this section will be devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.3
. We will first present some auxiliary results, then give the proof in §
6.6
.

6.3.
Mixing via shearing criterion

This Proposition uses the standard mixing mechanism called
mixing via shearing
which is at the heart of virtually every proof of mixing in parabolic flows, see for example
[
Ko75
,
Fay
,
Ulc07
,
Rav17
]
. We will use the following classical criterion that axiomatises the properties of a (partial) partition (into curves on which the shearing phenomenon happens and is controlled) needed to prove mixing via this mechanism.

Let us consider a special flow
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
I^{f}
built over an ergodic IET
T:I\to I
and under a roof function
f\in\mathcal{L}og(T)
. For any
x\in I
and
t>0
denote by
N(x,t)
the number of crossings of the roof of
I^{f}
(or, equivalently, the number of discrete iterations of the base transformation) undergone by the orbit segment starting from
(x,0)\in I^{f}
and flowing up to time
t
, i.e.

(22)

N(x,t):=\#\{s\in(0,t]:T^{f}_{s}(x,0)\in I\times\{0\}\}.

For any subinterval
J\subset I
, let us also introduce the notation

(23)

\underline{N}(J,t):=\min_{x\in J}N(x,t),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\overline{N}(J,t):=\max_{x\in J}N(x,t)

for the minimum and maximum of the function
x\mapsto N(x,t)
on the interval
J
respectively.

Lemma 6.5

(mixing via shearing criterion)
.

Let
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be a special flow over an IET
T
and with the roof function
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}^{\textrm{2+}}\left(T\right)
. Assume that for a sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
we have that there exists a sequence
(\mathcal{P}_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
of finite collections of disjoint intervals (called partitions) satisfying:

(M)

\displaystyle Leb(\mathcal{P}_{n})\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty;\vskip 3.69885pt

(D)

\displaystyle T^{j}J\cap End(T)=\emptyset\quad\text{for every}\ J\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\ \text{and}\ 0\leqslant j\leqslant\overline{N}(J,t_{n});\vskip 8.53581pt

(S1)

\displaystyle\min_{J\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{n}),\overline{N}(J,t_{n})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)||J|\to\infty\text{ as }n\to\infty;\vskip 17.07164pt

(S2)

\displaystyle\max_{J\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\frac{\max_{0\leqslant r\leqslant 2t_{n}}\max_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)||J|}{\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{n}),\overline{N}(J,t_{n})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|}\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty.\vskip 3.69885pt

Then
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is mixing along
(t_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
.

Even though our formulation is slightly different, this proposition has already been used in the literature, in particular by Kočergin, Fayad and Ulcigrai. Indeed, in
[
Fay
]
, the control in (
S1
) and (
S2
) is formally required for all
t_{n}/2\leqslant r\leqslant 2t_{n}
, although in the proof it is only used for all
\underline{N}(J,t_{n})\leqslant r\leqslant\overline{N}(J,t_{n})
. We refer also to
[
Ulc07
]
, where, although the criterion is not explicitly stated as such, the proof of mixing of
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is deduced from the existence of partitions which satisfy exactly these assumptions (M), (D), (S1) and (S2).

6.4.
Deviations of the number of discrete iterations in time
t
.

Fluctuations of the function
x\mapsto N(x,t)
defined in (
22
) of the previous §
6.3
can be reduced to the study of ergodic integrals of the special flow (or, equivalently, of locally Hamiltonian flows). Ergodic integrals of smooth functions over translation flows are well known to display
power deviations
of ergodic averages, a phenomenon discovered by Zorich (see
[
Zor97
]
) and proved by Forni
[
Fo02
]
. For locally Hamiltonian flows, a different proof (which uses a
correction
method inspired by
[
MMY05
]
) which yields this result for smooth observables was proved in
[
FrUl24
]
.

In the proof of mixing at resonant times we will need
the following quantitative estimate on the values of
N(x,t)
given by (
22
), which shows that, on sets of arbitrarily large measure of initial points, they display power deviations from the mean:

Lemma 6.6
.

For every irreducible permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}^{0}

there exists
0\leqslant\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}

such that, for almost every IET
T

with permutation
\pi
and every roof function
f\in\mathcal{L}og(T)
with mean value equal
1
and every
\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}<\gamma<1
, we have

\lim_{t\to+\infty}Leb\{x\in I:N(x,t)\in[t-t^{\gamma},t+t^{\gamma}]\}=1.

Remark 6.7
.

Here
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
depend only on the
Rauzy class

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\pi)
of the permutation
\pi
(see §
7.1.2
) and are the two top Lyapunov exponents of the Kontsevich-Zorich cocycle on the connected component of the stratum of Abelian differentials corresponding to
\mathcal{R}
.

The estimate can be deduced by the recent work
[
FrUl24
]
by the first and last author on deviation of ergodic averages of locally Hamiltonian flows by a simple smoothening argument, as we now explain.

For any measurable bounded map
\xi:I^{f}\to\mathbb{R}
let us consider
\varphi_{\xi}:I\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\varphi_{\xi}(x)=\int_{0}^{f(x)}\xi(x,r)dr\quad\text{for}\quad x\in I.

The following result that can be deduced from the results in
[
FrUl24
]
.

Proposition 6.8
.

For any irreducible
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}
and
0\leqslant\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}
as in Lemma
6.6
and Remark
6.7
, for almost every IET
T

with permutation
\pi

and every
\xi:I^{f}\to\mathbb{R}
measurable bounded map with zero mean such that

\varphi_{\xi}\in\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
,
for a.e.
(x,r)\in I^{f}
, we have

(24)

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,r))ds|}{\log t}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}<1.

This result shows that indeed, for
(x,r)
in a large measure set, the ergodic integral
\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,r))ds
has oscillations of size bounded by
O(t^{\alpha})
for any power
0<\alpha<1
such that
\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}<\alpha
.
We include the proof of this Proposition deducing it from the results proven in
[
FrUl24
]
in the Appendix
A
.

Let us show that the above Proposition
6.8
implies the desired control of oscillations of
N(x,t)
by a simple
thickening
argument.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Consider an IET
T
in the full measure set of IETs with permutation
\pi
for which Proposition
6.8
holds.
Fix
0<\epsilon<\inf_{x\in I}f(x)
and take
\xi=\xi_{\epsilon}:I^{f}\to\mathbb{R}
to be the (normalized) indicator function of a thickened cross section, given by

\xi_{\epsilon}(x,y):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\mathbf{1}_{I\times[0,\epsilon]}(x,y),\qquad(x,y)\in I^{f}.

Then for all
x\in I
and
t>0
, we have

\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,0))ds-1\leqslant N(x,t)\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,0))ds+1.

By Proposition
6.8
, applied to
\xi-1
, for a.e.
x\in I
, we have

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\int_{0}^{t}(\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,0))-1)ds|}{\log t}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}<\gamma.

It follows that
for a.e.
x\in I
, we have

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log|N(x,t)-t|}{\log t}<\gamma.

This shows our claim.
∎

6.5.
Estimates of the Birkhoff sums of the first derivative

Let us first estimate Birkhoff sums of
S_{r}(f^{\prime})
of the
derivative

f^{\prime}
of the roof function. The goal of this subsection is to prove the following estimates. Let us recall that with the notation introduced in §
5.1.2
, given the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}(x,r)
of
x
under
T
of length
r
, we denote by
m(x,r)
the minimal distance of
\mathcal{O}(x,r)
from the discontinuities of
T
.

Lemma 6.9
.

For almost every IET
T
and every
f\in\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}^{\textrm{2+}}\left(T\right)
, for every
\epsilon>0
there is a constant
M^{\prime}>0
such that for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in[0,1)
, we have

|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leqslant M^{\prime}r^{1+3\epsilon}+\frac{M^{\prime}}{m(x,r)}.

The set of IETs for which this Lemma holds contains the full measure set of IETs which are called
Roth type
in the work by Marmi, Moussa and Yoccoz
[
MMY05
]
. We will not give here the definition of Roth IETs (which we do not use directly), but we recall one of the consequences of being Roth type, which is proved in
[
MMY05
]
.

Given an IET
T
and a sequence
(I^{(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of subintervals of
I=[0,1)
, recall that the first return map of
T
to
I^{(k)}
is again an IET, of the same number of intervals if the sequence is chosen properly (in particular if it is the sequence given by Rauzy-Veech induction, see §
7.2.1
). We denote by
I^{(k)}_{j}
the continuity intervals of the induced map and by
\lambda^{(k)}_{j}=|I^{(k)}_{j}|
their lengths. Let us denote by

h^{(k)}_{j}
is the first return time of a (and hence every) point in
I^{(k)}_{j}
to
I^{(k)}
under
T
.

Lemma 6.10

(Roth type distortion control)
.

If
T
is Roth type, for every
0<\epsilon<1
there exists a constant
M:=M(\epsilon)>0

and there exists an inducing sequence of nested intervals
(I^{(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
the lengths
\lambda^{(k)}_{j}
and the return times
h^{(k)}_{j}
of the continuity intervals
I^{(k)}_{j}
of the induced IET on
I^{(k)}

satisfy the following controlled distortion bound:

\max_{j}h^{(k+1)}_{j}\leqslant M(\min_{j}h^{(k)}_{j})^{1+\epsilon}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\max_{j}\lambda^{(k)}_{j}\leqslant M(\min_{j}\lambda_{j}^{(k+1)})^{1-\epsilon}.

We include the proof of this Lemma deducing it from the results proven in
[
MMY05
]
in Appendix
B
.
Let us recall that one of the central results proved by Marmi-Moussa-Yoccoz in
[
MMY05
]
is that Roth-type IETs have full measure.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Assume that
T
belongs to the set of IETs which are of Roth type, which has full measure by
[
MMY05
]
. Let
(I^{(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence of inducing intervals given by Lemma
6.10
and denote as above by
\lambda^{(k)}_{j}
and
h^{(k)}_{j}
, for each
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
respectively, the length and the (constant) return time of the continuity interval
I^{(k)}_{j}
.

Choose
k
to be the smallest natural number such that
r<\min_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}
.

Case 1.
First suppose that the points in the orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,r)=\{T^{i}x\}_{i=0}^{r-1}
belong to
r
subsequent floors of the tower
over
I^{(k)}_{j}
with height
h^{(k)}_{j}
, whose floors are
\{T^{i}I^{(k)}_{j}:\ 0\leqslant i<h^{(k)}_{j}\}
.
Then (since
T
is an isometry), the points
\mathcal{O}(x,r)

are
\lambda^{(k)}_{j}
-separated, i.e., the minimum distance between any two of them is bounded below by
\lambda^{(k)}_{j}
.
Using classical Kočergin estimates based on the sum of inverses of an arithmetic progression, we have that if the points
(x_{i})_{i=0}^{N}\subset[0,1]
are
\delta
-separated for some
\delta>0
, then

\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{1}{x_{i}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{j\,\delta}\leqslant\frac{1}{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}x_{i}}+\frac{1+\log N}{\delta}.

This shows that, for
C_{sum}
as in (
2.1.4
), we have

|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leqslant C_{sum}\frac{1+\log r}{\min_{j}\lambda^{(k)}_{j}}+\frac{C_{sum}}{m(x,r)}+r\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Choose

j_{k}
such that
\lambda^{k-1}_{j_{k}}h^{k-1}_{j_{k}}\geqslant 1/d
(such
j_{k}
always exists since there are
d
towers whose total measure is
1
). Then, by using both inequalities of Lemma
6.10
(for lengths first and heights later), we have that

\min_{j}\lambda^{(k)}_{j}\geqslant\Big(\frac{\lambda^{k-1}_{j_{k}}}{M}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}}\geqslant\frac{1}{{(dMh^{k-1}_{j_{k}})}^{\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}}}\geqslant\frac{1}{M^{\frac{2}{1-\epsilon}}d^{\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}}{(\min_{j}h^{k-2}_{j}})^{\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}}.

Since
r\geqslant\min_{j}h_{j}^{k-1}\geqslant\min_{j}h^{k-2}_{j}
, (choosing
\epsilon<1/3
so that
\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}<1+3\epsilon
) we have that

|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leqslant C_{sum}(dM^{2})^{\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}}(1+\log r)r^{\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}+\frac{C_{sum}}{m(x,r)}+r\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leqslant M_{0}r^{1+3\epsilon}+\frac{M_{0}}{m(x,r)}

for a suitable constant
M_{0}>0
and
C_{sum}
as in (
2.1.4
).

Case 2.
Suppose that the orbit
\{T^{i}x\}_{i=0}^{r-1}
is not contained in any tower
\{T^{i}I^{(k)}_{j}:0\leqslant i<h^{(k)}_{j}\}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
. Since by choice of
k
,
r\leqslant\min_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}
, it follows that
\mathcal{O}(x,r)
can be decomposed into at most two towers over intervals of
I^{(k)}
, to each of which we can apply the arguments from Case 1. This gives

|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leqslant 2M_{0}r^{1+3\epsilon}+\frac{2M_{0}}{m(x,r)},

which, setting
M^{\prime}:=2M_{0}
, completes the proof.
∎

6.6.
Mixing at resonant times proof

In this section we prove Proposition
6.3
, i.e. we show that for a full measure set of IETs, times comparable with resonant rigid times, i.e. times of the form
k_{m}q_{m}
, where
q_{m}
is a rigidity time for the IET and
k_{m}
sufficiently large, are mixing times for logarithmic special flows over the IET.

6.6.1.
The full measure set of IETs

Denote by
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{d}\subset\mathcal{I}_{d}
the union over permutations
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}
of the full measure sets of IETs with permutation
\pi
for which Lemma
6.6
and Proposition
6.8
controlling fluctuations of the function
N(x,t)
given by (
22
) and of ergodic integrals hold. By construction,
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{d}
has full measure in
\mathcal{I}_{d}
.
The full measure set
\mathcal{F}_{d}
of IETs in
\mathcal{I}_{d}
for which we will prove Proposition
6.3
is given by intersecting
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{d}
with the full measure set of
\mathcal{I}_{d}
(containing Roth type IETs) for which Lemma
6.9
holds.

6.6.2.
Choices of parameters and notation

If for each
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}
we denote by
\lambda_{1}(\pi),\lambda_{2}(\pi)
the exponents in Lemma
6.6
and Proposition
6.8
(namely the Lyapunov exponents of the Rauzy class
\mathcal{R}
containing
\pi
, see Remark
6.7
), since
\lambda_{2}(\pi)/\lambda_{1}(\pi)<1
for any
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}
, we can choose
0<\gamma<1
and
\epsilon>0
such that for each
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}

\max_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{d}}\lambda_{2}(\pi)/\lambda_{1}(\pi)<\gamma<1,\qquad 0<\epsilon<\gamma/6,\qquad\gamma(1+3\epsilon)<1.

We will for simplicity assume throughout this section (without loss of generality) that
\int_{0}^{1}f(x)dx=1
. We will also denote by
\delta_{m}

(25)

\delta_{m}:=\frac{1}{\log k_{m}}\to 0,

where
(k_{m}q_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
(\underline{\epsilon},\underline{k})
-resonant times for
T
.
By definition, for every
m\geqslant 1
there exists an
\epsilon^{2}_{m}
-rigid tower (
\epsilon_{m}\leqslant 1/k_{m}
) of intervals
\mathcal{T}_{m}
such that
q_{m}
is its height and
Leb(\mathcal{T}_{m})\geqslant 1-\epsilon_{m}
.
Denote the base of
\mathcal{T}_{m}
by
I_{m}=[a_{m},b_{m}]
.

6.6.3.
Variation of
N(x,t_{m})
in the large tower.

For the proof of the proposition we will first need a lemma which gives good control on
N(x,t_{m})
(as defined by (
22
)) for most of the points in the tower
\mathcal{T}_{m}
:

Lemma 6.11

(controlled deviations floors)
.

Suppose that
t\in[k_{m}q_{m},Ck_{m}q_{m}]
.
Let
J
be a floor of the tower

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{m}-1}T^{j}\left[a_{m}+\frac{\delta_{m}}{q_{m}},b_{m}-\frac{\delta_{m}}{q_{m}}\right]

for which we have that
N(x_{0},t)\in[t-t^{\gamma},t+t^{\gamma}]
for some
x_{0}\in J
. Then

(26)

N(x,t)\in[t-2t^{\gamma},t+2t^{\gamma}]\quad\text{for\ all}\ x\in J.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of the Lemma.

Proof of Lemma
6.11
.

Let
c:=\inf_{x\in[0,1)}f(x)>0
. By definition,

\displaystyle c|N(x,t)-N(x_{0},t)|\leqslant|S_{N(x,t)-N(x_{0},t)}(f)(T^{N(x_{0},t)}x)|=|S_{N(x,t)}(f)(x)-S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x)|.

By the definition of
N
, for all
y\in[0,1)
, we have

0\leqslant t-S_{N(y,t)}f(y)<f(T^{N(y,t)}y).

It follows that

|S_{N(x,t)}f(x)-S_{N(x_{0},t)}f(x_{0})|\leqslant f(T^{N(x,t)}(x))+f(T^{N(x_{0},t)}(x_{0})).

Therefore,

(27)

c|N(x,t)-N(x_{0},t)|\leqslant f(T^{N(x,t)}(x))+f(T^{N(x_{0},t)}(x_{0}))+|S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x_{0})-S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x)|.

Note that by the rough bound
N(y,t)\leqslant c^{-1}t\leqslant c^{-1}Ck_{m}q_{m}
for all
y\in J
. Moreover, as the tower
\mathcal{T}_{m}
is
\epsilon^{2}_{m}
-rigid and
\epsilon_{m}\leqslant\frac{1}{k_{m}}
, for all
y\in J
we have
|T^{q_{m}}y-y|<\frac{\epsilon^{2}_{m}}{q_{m}}\leqslant\frac{1}{k^{2}_{m}q_{m}}
. Repeating it
\frac{C}{c}k_{m}
-times,
we have that for all
0\leqslant N\leqslant\frac{C}{c}k_{m}{q_{m}}
,
T^{N}y
is in the tower
\mathcal{T}_{m}
and at least
\frac{\delta_{m}}{q_{m}}-\frac{C}{ck_{m}q_{m}}
distant from the ends of the levels of
\mathcal{T}_{m}
. Since
\delta_{m}=\frac{1}{\log k_{m}}
, we have
\frac{\delta_{m}}{q_{m}}-\frac{C}{ck_{m}q_{m}}\geqslant\frac{\delta_{m}}{2q_{m}}
. It follows that, for
C_{sum}
as in (
2.1.4
),

(28)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f(T^{N}(y))&\leqslant C_{sum}\log(\frac{2q_{m}}{\delta_{m}})+\|g\|_{sup}\leqslant C_{sum}\log(2q_{m}\log k_{m})+\|g\|_{sup}\\
&<C_{sum}\log(k_{m}q_{m})\leqslant C_{sum}\log t\end{aligned}

uniformly for
y\in J
. Moreover, by Lemma
6.9
(which we can apply since we are assuming that
T
belongs to the set
\mathcal{F}_{d}
for which the conclusion of the Lemma applies, see §
6.6.1
),

\displaystyle|S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x_{0})-S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f^{\prime})(\theta)|q_{m}^{-1}\leqslant\frac{M^{\prime}}{q_{m}}\left((c^{-1}Ck_{m}q_{m})^{1+3\epsilon}+\frac{2q_{m}}{\delta_{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}((k_{m}q_{m})^{3\epsilon}\log\log q_{m}+\log k_{m})<(k_{m}q_{m})^{\gamma/2}\leqslant t^{\gamma/2},

where we use
\epsilon
-Roth type,
3\epsilon<\gamma/2
, the fact that
\theta\in J
and
k_{m}\leqslant\log\log q_{m}
.
Finally, in view of (
27
) and (
28
), this gives

\displaystyle|N(x,t)-N(x_{0},t)|

\displaystyle\leqslant c^{-1}(f(T^{N(x,t)}(x))+f(T^{N(x_{0},t)}(x_{0}))+|S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x_{0})-S_{N(x_{0},t)}(f)(x)|)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2C_{sum}c^{-1}\log t+c^{-1}t^{\gamma/2}<t^{\gamma}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

6.6.4.
Estimates of Birkhoff sums of the second derivative.

We will also need one more lemma which gives good control for the Birkhoff sums of the second (and hence the first) derivative.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\ell\in[\frac{1}{2}k_{m}q_{m},2Ck_{m}q_{m}]
. Then for every
x\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}
,

(29)

\underline{c}k_{m}q_{m}^{2}\leqslant S_{\ell}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)\leqslant\overline{c}k_{m}\delta_{m}^{-2}q_{m}^{2},

where
\underline{c}:={C_{min}/16}>0
and
\overline{c}:=20CC_{sum}
with

{C_{min}:=\min\{C^{\pm}_{i}:C^{\pm}_{i}>0\}.}

Proof.

First note that if the points
(x_{i})_{i=0}^{N}\subset[0,1]
are
\delta
-separated for some
\delta>0
, then

\frac{1}{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}x^{2}_{i}}\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{1}{x^{2}_{i}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}x^{2}_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{j^{2}\,\delta^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\min_{0\leqslant i\leqslant N}x^{2}_{i}}+\frac{2}{\delta^{2}}.

It follows that for every
x\in[0,1)
and every natural
r
if the orbit
\{T^{i}x\}_{i=0}^{r-1}
is
\delta
-separated then

(30)

{\frac{C_{min}}{m_{e}(x,r)^{2}}}-r\|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{sup}\leqslant S_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)\leqslant\frac{C_{sum}}{m(x,r)^{2}}+\frac{2C_{sum}}{\delta^{2}}+r\|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{sup},

where
m(x,r)
is the closest visit to all singularities (see §
5.1.2
) and
m_{e}(x,r)
is the closest visit to essential singularities defined as follows.
Due to the geometric origin of the roof function
f
(it is the first return time function for a flow on a surface of genus at least
2
), there are exactly two zero coefficients, namely
C^{+}_{i_{+}}
and
C^{-}_{i_{-}}
for some
0\leqslant i_{+}<d
and
1\leqslant i_{-}\leqslant d
. The closest visit to the essential singularities is then defined by

\displaystyle m_{e}(x,r):

\displaystyle=\min\big\{\{m_{i}^{+}(x,r):\ 0\leqslant i<d,\ i\neq i_{+}\}\cup\{m_{i}^{-}(x,r):\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant d,\ i\neq i_{-}\}\big\}

\displaystyle=\min\big\{\{m_{i}^{+}(x,r):\ 0\leqslant i<d,\ C^{+}_{i}>0\}\cup\{m_{i}^{-}(x,r):\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant d,\ C^{-}_{i}>0\}\big\}.

Let
i_{0}:=\pi^{-1}(1)-1
and
i_{d}:=\pi^{-1}(d)
, that is,
T\beta{i_{0}}=\beta_{0}
and
\beta{i_{d}}
is the right endpoint of the interval translated by
T
to the last exchanged interval, whose right endpoint is
\beta_{d}
. Then
i_{+}\in\{0,i_{0}\}
and
i_{-}\in\{d,i_{d}\}
.

Suppose that
x
is such that its orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{m})\subset\mathcal{T}_{m}
. Since
T\beta_{i_{0}}=\beta_{0}
, we obtain

|m^{+}_{i_{0}}(x,q_{m})-m^{+}_{0}(x,q_{m})|\leqslant|T^{q_{m}}x-x|\leqslant\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{q_{m}}\leqslant\frac{1}{q_{m}}.

A similar argument shows also that
|m^{-}_{i_{d}}(x,q_{m})-m^{-}_{d}(x,q_{m})|\leqslant 1/q_{m}
. Since
C^{+}_{0}
or
C^{+}_{i_{0}}
is positive, and
C^{-}_{d}
or
C^{-}_{i_{d}}
is positive, it follows that

m_{e}(x,q_{m})\leqslant m(x,q_{m})+\frac{1}{q_{m}}\leqslant\frac{2}{q_{m}}.

Lower bound.

As
f^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0
,
the lower bound follows from the fact that

S_{\ell}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)\geqslant S_{[\frac{k_{m}}{2}]q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{[\frac{k_{m}}{2}]-1}S_{q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(T^{jq_{m}}x)

and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{jq_{m}}x,q_{m})\subset\mathcal{T}_{m}
for all
0\leqslant j<k_{m}/2
. Then
m_{e}(T^{jq_{m}}x,q_{m})\leqslant\frac{2}{q_{m}}
and, by (
30
), we have

{S_{q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(T^{jq_{m}}x)\geqslant\frac{C_{min}}{4}q_{m}^{2}-\|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{sup}q_{m}\geqslant 2\underline{c}q^{2}_{m},}

which gives the lower bound.

Upper bound.

As
f^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0
, for the upper bound we similarly write

S_{\ell}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)\leqslant S_{(2Ck_{m}+1)q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(x_{0})=\sum_{j=0}^{2Ck_{m}}S_{q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(T^{jq_{m}}x_{0}),

where
x_{0}
is the first backward visit (to the base of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}
) point of the orbit of
x
.
We then use the fact that points
\{T^{i}(T^{jq_{m}}x_{0})\}_{0\leqslant i<q_{m}}
are
\frac{1}{2q_{m}}
separated and by the assumption
x\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}
and the rigidity condition it follows that
m(T^{jq_{m}}x_{0},q_{m})\geqslant\frac{\delta_{m}}{2q_{m}}
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant 2Ck_{m}
. In view of (
30
), this gives

\displaystyle S_{\ell}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{2Ck_{m}}S_{q_{m}}(f^{\prime\prime})(T^{jq_{m}}x_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant 3Ck_{m}\left(4C_{sum}q^{2}_{m}\delta^{-2}_{m}+8C_{sum}q_{m}^{2}+\|g^{\prime\prime}\|_{sup}q_{m}\right)\leqslant\overline{c}k_{m}\delta_{m}^{-2}q_{m}^{2}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

6.7.
Construction of mixing partitions

We can now conclude the proof that resonant rigid times are mixing (i.e. prove Proposition
6.3
), using the estimates proved and the
mixing via shearing
criterion (i.e. of Lemma
6.5
).

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Let us assume that
T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}
belongs to the full measure set
\mathcal{F}_{d}
defined in §
6.6.1
.
Let us define a partition of
[0,1]
for which we can verify the assumptions of the
mixing via shearing
(Lemma
6.5
) criterion.
For this, given
t_{m}\in[k_{m}q_{m},Ck_{m}q_{m}]
let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m}
be the partition into floors of the tower
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}
, namely into intervals
\{T^{j}(I^{\prime}_{m})\}_{0\leqslant j<q_{m}}
, where
I^{\prime}_{m}
is the base of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{m}
. We will only consider the floors
F_{j}^{m}:=T^{j}(I^{\prime}_{m})
which have a non-empty intersection with the set given by Lemma
6.6
, namely such that

(31)

F_{j}^{m}\cap\{x\in[0,1)\;:\;N(x,t_{m})\in[t_{m}-t_{m}^{\gamma},t_{m}+t_{m}^{\gamma}]\}\neq\emptyset.

We will call these floors
good
and we denote by
\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}_{m}
the partial partitions obtained keeping only these good intervals. We claim that good intervals constitute the majority of the intervals, or in other words,
Leb(\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}_{m})\to 1
as
m\to\infty
.
Indeed, since by construction the union of all (good) intervals in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}_{m}
covers the set
\{x\in[0,1)\;:\;N(x,t_{m})\in[t_{m}-t_{m}^{\gamma},t_{m}+t_{m}^{\gamma}]\}
,
this follows
from the estimate given by Lemma
6.6
. Note that then by Lemma
6.11
, if a floor
F^{m}_{j}
is good, then for all
x\in F^{m}_{j}
we have a similar control of
N(x,t_{m})
, see (
26
).
Moreover, by the construction of
I^{\prime}_{m}
, it follows that for every interval
J\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m}
, no discontinuity lies inside
T^{j}J
for
j\leqslant 2Ck_{m}q_{m}
. This together with the bound

\overline{N}(J,t_{m}):=\max_{x\in J}N(x,t_{m})\leqslant 2t_{m}\leqslant 2Ck_{m}q_{m},

gives the condition (
D
).
Our partition
\mathcal{P}_{m}
will be a refinement of the partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m}
and note that the property (
D
) is valid also for any such refinement.
By Lemma
6.11
, the good floors not only intersect, but are fully contained in the above sets. We now consider three separate cases, that we define using the quantities
\underline{N}(x,t)
and
\overline{N}(x,t)
given as in (
23
).

Case 0.
Suppose that
J
is a subinterval of an atom of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m}
such that its length is in
[\frac{\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}},\frac{2\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}}]
and

\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\geqslant\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}.

Then, we have

\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\cdot|J|\geqslant\overline{c}\delta_{m}^{5}k_{m}\to\infty,

as
\delta_{m}=(\log k_{m})^{-1}
. This gives (
S1
). Moreover, by Lemma
6.12
,

\frac{\max_{r\leqslant 2t_{m}}\max_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(x)||J|}{\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|}\leqslant\frac{\overline{c}k_{m}\delta_{m}^{-2}q_{m}^{2}\cdot\delta_{m}^{4}q_{m}^{-1}}{\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}}=\delta_{m}\to 0,

and so (
S2
) also holds.

In the remaining cases, we will construct
{\mathcal{P}_{m}}
as a refinement of
{\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m}}
.
Let
J
be an atom of the partition
{\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m}}
. We will consider two cases depending on the size of the first derivative on
J
:

Case I:
Suppose now that

\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\geqslant\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}.

In this case we further divide
J
(in an arbitrary way) into intervals
\{J_{i}\}
of length in
[\frac{\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}},\frac{2\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}}]
.
In this case the atoms of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{m}
coming from
J
will be the intervals
\{J_{i}\}
.
In view of Case 0, all these new atoms satisfy (
S1
) and (
S2
).

Case II:
Suppose finally that

\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leqslant\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}.

Let
r_{J}\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]
and
x_{J}\in J
be such that

\min_{r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]}\min_{x\in J}|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|=|S_{r_{J}}(f^{\prime})(x_{J})|\leqslant\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}.

Take any
r\in[\underline{N}(J,t_{m}),\overline{N}(J,t_{m})]
. By the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m}
and
t_{m}\leqslant Ck_{m}q_{m}
, we have
|r-r_{J}|<4t_{m}^{\gamma}\leqslant 4C(k_{m}q_{m})^{\gamma}
. Then, by the fact that
T
is
\epsilon
-Roth and using Lemma
6.9
, we get

|S_{r-r_{J}}{f^{\prime}}(T^{r_{J}}x_{J})|\leqslant M^{\prime}|r-r_{J}|^{1+3\epsilon}+M^{\prime}\frac{2q_{m}}{\delta_{m}}\leqslant 16C^{2}M^{\prime}(k_{m}q_{m})^{\gamma(1+3\epsilon)}+2M^{\prime}q_{m}\log k_{m}.

As
\gamma(1+3\epsilon)<1
, this gives

|S_{r-r_{J}}{f^{\prime}}(T^{r_{J}}x_{J})|\leqslant\overline{c}\frac{k_{m}}{\log k_{m}}q_{m}=\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m},

so

|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x_{J})|\leqslant|S_{r_{J}}(f^{\prime})(x_{J})|+|S_{r-r_{J}}{f^{\prime}}(T^{r_{J}}x_{J})|\leqslant 2\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}.

We remove from
J
the
\frac{3\overline{c}\delta_{m}}{\underline{c}q_{m}}
neighborhood of
x_{J}
. We denote the remaining intervals in
J
by
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
. The following holds for both
\bar{J}=J_{1},J_{2}
. Using Taylor expansion and lower bounds on second derivative in Lemma
6.12
, we get that for every
x\in\bar{J}
and every

r\in[\underline{N}(\bar{J},t_{m}),\overline{N}(\bar{J},t_{m})]\subset[t_{m}/2,2t_{m}]\subset[\frac{1}{2}k_{m}q_{m},2Ck_{m}q_{m}],

we have

\displaystyle|S_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|

\displaystyle=|S_{r}{f^{\prime}}(x_{J})+S_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(\theta)(x-x_{J})|\geqslant|S_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(\theta)||x-x_{J}|-|S_{r}{f^{\prime}}(x_{J})|

\displaystyle\geqslant\underline{c}k_{m}q_{m}^{2}\frac{3\overline{c}\delta_{m}}{\underline{c}q_{m}}-2\overline{c}\delta_{m}k_{m}q_{m}=\overline{c}k_{m}q_{m}\delta_{m}.

In this case we further divide
\bar{J}
(in an arbitrary way) into intervals
\{\bar{J}_{i}\}
of length in
[\frac{\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}},\frac{2\delta_{m}^{4}}{q_{m}}]
.
In this case the atoms of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{m}
coming from
\bar{J}
will be the intervals
\{\bar{J}_{i}\}
.
In view of Case 0, all these new atoms satisfy (
S1
) and (
S2
).

Final estimate.
In both cases (Case I and Case II), for the atoms of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{m}
that we defined in each case, we get

Leb(\mathcal{P}_{m})\geqslant Leb(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m})-q_{m}\frac{6\overline{c}\delta_{m}}{\underline{c}q_{m}}=Leb(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{m})-\frac{6\overline{c}}{\underline{c}}\delta_{m}\to 1\text{ as }m\to\infty.

Therefore, the condition (
M
) of the
mixing via shearing
criterion (Lemma
6.5
) holds. Thus we can apply the criterion, which concludes the proof that resonant times are mixing.
∎

7.
Properties of Rauzy-Veech induction

To prove the two
technical propositions that we left to prove (Proposition
5.6
to conclude the proof of singularity of the spectrum and Proposition
6.4
to conclude the proof of spectral disjointness), we will use as a tool Rauzy-Veech induction and exploit some of the stochastic properties that the induction features.
In §
7.1
and §
7.2
, we first recall some basic terminology and features of Rauzy-Veech induction and its invertible extension. In §
7.3
, we then recall some features which are at the heart of some of the statistical and ergodic properties of the induction (in particular, the
local product structure
given by the coordinates
\lambda
and
\tau
in §
7.3.1
and the Markovian structure of the partitions given by prescribing finitely many iterates types, see §
7.3.2
).
Finally in §
7.4
, we recall an estimate known as
Kerckhoff’s lemma
and some of its consequences on distortion properties of Markov partitions.

7.1.
Rauzy-Veech induction for IETs

The Rauzy-Veech algorithm, by now a classical and powerful tool to study IETs, was originally introduced and developed in the works by Rauzy and Veech
[
Rau
,
Vee82
]
. We recall here only some basic definitions needed in the rest of this paper. For more details on the definition of the algorithm as well as a different notation for permutations (using an
alphabet

\mathcal{A}
and a
pair
of permutations to keep track of the
labels
of intervals, which is important for some of the properties of the induction), we refer e.g. to lecture notes by Yoccoz
[
Yoc10
]
or Viana
[
Via
]
.

7.1.1.
Basic algorithm on IETs.

The Rauzy-Veech induction algorithm associates to a.e. IET, a sequence of IETs which are induced transformations of
T
onto a sequence of nested subintervals
I^{(n)}
contained in
[0,1]
. These are chosen so that the induced transformations are again IETs of the same number
d
of exchanged intervals.
If
I^{\prime}\subset I:=[0,1]
denotes the subinterval associated to one step of the algorithm,
I^{\prime}
is defined as follows: let
I^{t}_{d}:=I_{d}=[\beta_{d-1},1]
(where
t
stands for
top
last) be the last (i.e. right-most) exchanged subinterval and let
j_{d}:=\pi^{-1}(d)
be the index such that
T(I_{j_{d}})
has
1
as endpoint, i.e.
I_{d}^{\prime}:=I_{j_{d}}
is the interval which becomes last after the exchange; equivalently,
I^{b}_{d}:=T(I_{j_{d}})
is the last (i.e. rightmost) exchanged interval for the inverse
T^{-1}
. Then, we set:

I^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}[0,1]\backslash I^{b}_{d},&\text{if}\ |I^{t}_{d}|>|I^{b}_{d}|\ \qquad\text{(case\ top\ or\ type\ 0),}\\
[0,1]\backslash I^{t}_{d},&\text{if}\ |I^{b}_{d}|>|I^{t}_{d}|\qquad\text{(case\ bottom\ or\ type\ 1).}\end{cases}

The
Rauzy-Veech map

\mathcal{V}
then associates to
T=(\pi,\lambda)
such that
\lambda_{d}\neq\lambda_{j_{d}}
(so that
|I^{t}_{d}|\neq|I^{b}_{d}|
)
the IET
\mathcal{V}(T)=\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda)
obtained by renormalizing
T^{\prime}
by
|I^{\prime}|
so that the renormalized IET is again defined on a unit interval. If
|I^{t}_{d}|=|I^{b}_{d}|
, the induction is not defined.

7.1.2.
Parameter spaces of the Rauzy-Veech map.

Interval exchanges
T=(\pi,\lambda)
on the unit interval
I=[0,1]
can be parametrized by the permutation
\pi
and a length vector
\lambda
which belongs to the simplex

(32)

\Delta_{d}:=\left\{(\lambda_{1},\dots\lambda_{d})\ \Big|\quad\lambda_{i}\geqslant 0,{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}}=1\right\}.

Recall that a
Rauzy class
is a non-empty minimal (with respect to inclusion) subset
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{d}^{0}

of irreducible permutations (see §
2.1.1
for the definition of irreducible)
such that
\Delta_{d}\times\mathcal{R}
is invariant under the Rauzy-Veech map
\mathcal{V}
. For any irreducible
\pi_{0}
, we denote by
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0})
the Rauzy class containing
\pi_{0}
. Thus,
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0})

is the subset of all permutations
\pi^{\prime}
which appear as permutations of an IET
T^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime})
in the orbit under
\mathcal{V}
of some IET
(\pi_{0},{\lambda})
with initial permutation
\pi_{0}
. The associated Rauzy-
graph
, also denoted by
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{0})
, has these permutations as vertices, with an arrow
\gamma
starting at
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
and labeled top, or
0
(resp. bottom or
1
) iff there is a
\lambda
of type
0
(resp. type
1
) such that
\mathcal{V}(\pi,{\lambda})=(\pi^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime})
.

The domain of definition of the Rauzy-Veech map
\mathcal{V}
is a full Lebesgue measure subset of the space

(33)

X:=X(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}\times\Delta_{d}=\{(\pi,\lambda)\ |\ \pi\in\mathcal{R},\lambda\in\Delta_{d}\},

corresponding to those IETs for which the algorithm is defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, more precisely, the full measure set of IETs which satisfy the Keane condition.
Recall that an IET
T
satisfies the
Keane condition
if (recalling that
End(T)
denotes the set of end points of the IET
T
, see §
2.1.1
),

T^{m}\beta_{i}\neq{\beta}_{j}
for all
m\geqslant 1
and for all
i,j\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
except
j=0
with
m=1
.
Keane
[
Kea
]
showed that an IET with an irreducible permutation that satisfies the Keane condition is
minimal
.
Veech proved in
[
Vee82
]
that
\mathcal{V}
admits an invariant measure
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
on
X(\mathcal{R})
which is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure, but infinite.
Zorich showed in
[
Zor96
]
that one can accelerate
36
36

36

The acceleration of a map is obtained a.e.-defining an integer valued function
z(T)
which gives the return time to an appropriate section. The accelerated map is then given by
\mathcal{Z}(T):=\mathcal{V}^{z(T)}(T)
.
the map
\mathcal{V}
in order to obtain a map
\mathcal{Z}
, which we call
Zorich map
, that admits a
finite
invariant measure
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Let us also recall that both
\mathcal{V}
and its acceleration
\mathcal{Z}
are
ergodic
with respect to
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
respectively
[
Vee82
,
Zor96
]
.

7.1.3.
Matrix products indexed by paths

Given an IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
in the domain of
\mathcal{V}
, write
\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda)=(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
, let
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
be the
type
of
(\pi,\lambda)
(see §
7.1.1
), then denote by
\gamma
the corresponding arrow in the Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
starting from
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
. Then

\lambda^{\prime}=\frac{(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\lambda}{|(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\lambda|},\quad\text{where}\ B_{\gamma}\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})

is a non-negative matrix with entries
0
or
1
and with
1
on the diagonal and only one
1
off the diagonal and
B^{*}
denotes the transpose of the matrix
B
. If
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\ldots\gamma_{n}
is a path in the Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
which starts at
\pi_{0}
and ends at
\pi_{n}
obtained concatenating the arrows
\gamma_{i}
, associate to
\gamma

the product matrix
B_{\gamma}=B_{\gamma_{n}}B_{\gamma_{n-1}}\cdots B_{\gamma_{1}}
. Then

(34)

\mathcal{V}^{n}\left(\pi_{0},\frac{B^{*}_{\gamma}\lambda}{\left|B^{*}_{\gamma}\lambda\right|}\right)=(\pi_{n},\lambda),\quad\text{where}\ B^{*}_{\gamma}=(B_{\gamma_{n}}B_{\gamma_{n-1}}\cdots B_{\gamma_{1}})^{*}=B^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots B^{*}_{\gamma_{n-1}}B^{*}_{\gamma_{n}}.

If
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\ldots\gamma_{n}
