is a path in the Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
encoding the first
n
steps of the Rauzy-Veech induction for an IET
(\pi_{0},\lambda)
, then

(35)

\mathcal{V}^{n}(\pi_{0},\lambda)=\left(\pi_{n},\frac{(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\lambda}{\left|(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\lambda\right|}\right).

7.1.4.
Length-cylinder simplices

Given a non-negative matrix
B
in
SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
, let us denote by

(36)

\Delta_{B}:=B^{*}\Delta_{d}=\left\{\frac{B^{*}\lambda}{|B^{*}\lambda|},\quad\lambda\in\Delta_{d}\right\}\subset\Delta_{d}

the simplex image of
\Delta_{d}
by the projective action of
B
. For short, if
B=B_{\gamma}
for some path
\gamma
in the Rauzy graph starting at
\pi
, we write

(37)

\Delta_{\gamma}:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}=\{\pi\}\times B^{*}_{\gamma}(\Delta_{d}).

Then one can show that if
(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma}
, then the types of the first
n
steps of the Rauzy-Veech induction are given by
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
and

B_{\gamma}^{*}

is given by (
34
).

7.2.
Invertible Rauzy-Veech induction

The
natural extensions

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
, respectively of the Rauzy-Veech map
\mathcal{V}
and Zorich map
\mathcal{Z}
, also known as
invertible Rauzy-Veech
and
invertible Zorich
induction, are invertible maps
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
defined on a domain
\widehat{X}
which are extensions of
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{Z}
respectively, i.e. such that there exists a projection
p\colon\widehat{X}\rightarrow X
for which
p\widehat{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{V}p
and
p\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathcal{Z}p
.

7.2.1.
Parameter spaces of the invertible Rauzy-Veech and Zorich map

To define the domain of the
natural extensions
of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
, for each
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
let
\Theta_{\pi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the polyhedral cones given by the inequalities

\Theta_{\pi}:=\left\{{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \Big|\quad\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}>0,\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{\pi^{-1}i}<0,\,k=1,\dots,d-1\right\},

and let
\widehat{X}
be the following space of triples (that admits a geometric interpretation in terms of the space of zippered rectangles, see for example
[
Yoc10
,
Via
]
, but the latter will play no role in the present paper):

\widehat{X}:=\widehat{X}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{\pi}=\{\,(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})\ |\quad\pi\in\mathcal{R},\,{\lambda}\in\Delta_{d},\,{\tau}\in\Theta_{\pi}\}.

The action of the invertible extension
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
on triples
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}
is obtained by extending the projective action of
\mathcal{V}
as follows. If we write
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
, we have that
(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda)
(so that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
is an extension of
\mathcal{V}
) and

\tau^{\prime}=\frac{(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\tau}{|(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\lambda|},\quad\text{where}\ B^{*}_{\gamma}\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})

is the transpose of the matrix
B_{\gamma}
associated to the arrow in the Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
starting from
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
(see §
7.1.3
).

7.2.2.
Invariant measures

One can show that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
preserves an infinite invariant measure
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
on
\widehat{X}
, cf.
[
Zor96
,
Vee82
,
Via
]
. In fact,
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
restricted to
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{\pi}
is the product of
{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and the Lebesgue measure restricted to the open cone
\Theta_{\pi}
. Furthermore, on
\widehat{X}
one can define the real–valued function
Area(\cdot)
associates to
(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})
given by

Area(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau}):=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lambda_{k}\,(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\tau_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{\pi(k)-1}\tau_{\pi^{-1}i})

(called this way since it gives geometrically the area of the corresponding zippered rectangle).
Given any measurable subset
E\subset\widehat{X}
,
we will denote by
E^{(1)}
the intersection of
E
with the level set
Area^{-1}(1)
of the area function, namely

E^{(1)}=\{\,(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})\in E\ |\ \ Area(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=1\}.

In particular, this notation applies to
\widehat{X}
, so

\widehat{X}^{(1)}:=\widehat{X}\cap Area^{-1}(1)=\{\,(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})\,|\quad\pi\in\mathcal{R},\,{\lambda}\in\Delta_{d},\,{\tau}\in\Theta_{\pi},\,Area(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})=1\},

The projection
p
is then simply the forgetful map

p:\widehat{X}\rightarrow X
given by
p(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})=(\pi,{\lambda})
.

Since the renormalization
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
preserves
Area
, we can restrict it to
\widehat{X}^{(1)}
and get a well defined map

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}^{(1)}\to\widehat{X}^{(1)}
.
Moreover, the projectivization of the measure
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, denoted by
\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, is a finite
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
-invariant measure on
\widehat{X}^{(1)}
and is also ergodic (see e.g.
[
Via
]
for details).
In fact, the projectivization
\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is defined as follows

(38)

\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}(B):=\frac{\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\bigcup_{t\in{[0,1]}}tB)}{\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\bigcup_{t\in{[0,1]}}t\widehat{X}^{(1)})}\quad\text{ for {all} measurable}\quad B\subset\widehat{X}^{(1)},

where
t(\pi,\lambda,\tau):=(\pi,\lambda,t\tau)
.
The push-forward
p_{*}{\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
by the projection
p
(i.e. the measure such that
p_{*}{\widehat{\mu}}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}(A)={\widehat{\mu}}^{(1)}_{Z}(p^{-1}A)
for every measurable set
A\subset X
) equals
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
(see again
[
Via
]
).

7.2.3.
Accelerations of the Rauzy-Veech map.

One can consider
accelerations
of the (invertible) Zorich map
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
by
considering Poincaré first return map as follows. Fix a subset
E\subset\widehat{X}^{(1)}

of positive
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}
-measure. By the ergodicity of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
, for the orbit of
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}
-almost every
(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
under
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
visits
E
infinitely often.
The corresponding acceleration of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
is denoted by
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}
and it is a map
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}:\widehat{X}^{(1)}\to E
defined a.e. on
\widehat{X}^{(1)}
as follows:

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)=\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n_{1}}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
if
n_{1}\geqslant 1
is the first times when the forward orbit of
(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
hits
E
, i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n_{1}}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)\in E
.
If
\{n_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
denotes the sequence of subsequent visits to
E
for a typical
(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
(so that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)\in E
for all
\ell
and no other iterate
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
belongs to
E
), then

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{\ell}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau):=\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

We say that a sequence
\{n_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
(lengths) balanced times
for
T
if there exists
\nu>1

such that the lengths satisfy

\frac{1}{\nu}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{i}^{(n_{l})}}{\lambda_{j}^{(n_{l})}}\leqslant\nu\quad\text{ for\ all\ }1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d,\ \text{ and\ all\ }l\in\mathbb{N}.

One can see that if the subset
E
is of the form
E=\{\pi\}\times K\times Y
, where
K\subset\Delta_{d}
is precompact and
Y\subset\Theta_{\pi}
, then the acceleration
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}
is balanced. Recall that a subset
K\subset\Delta_{d}
is
precompact
if its closure
\overline{K}\subset\operatorname{Int}\Delta_{d}
(then
\overline{K}
is indeed compact with respect to the Hilbert metric on
\Delta_{d}
, which we will not use in this paper).

7.3.
Symbolic cylinders and Markovian structure

We describe now some features of the Rauzy-Veech and Zorich maps on parameter space, which are manifestation of the intrinsic hyperbolicity of the algorithm.

7.3.1.
Local product structure

A key observation that makes the coordinates
\pi,\lambda,\tau
on the domain
\widehat{X}
of the natural extension
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
of Rauzy-Veech induction particularly useful is the following form of (
local
)
product structure
.
Notice first that by definition of the elementary step of Rauzy-Veech induction, only the permutation
\pi
and the vector
\lambda
determine whether the basic step of the (invertible) Rauzy-Veech induction is of type
top
or
bottom
(and hence the formula which gives the image of
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
).
Similarly, the
backward
basic step of the
inverse

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}
of the (invertible) Rauzy-Veech induction
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
is determined by
\pi
and
\tau
only:

Remark 7.1
.

If
(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
, then one can know whether

(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
and hence the Rauzy-Veech operation performed by
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
on
(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
was of type
top
(resp.
bottom
) from the vector
\tau
only. More precisely, if

(39)

\tau_{1}+\dots+\tau_{d}<0\qquad\text{(respectively \ $>0$)},

the previous move was of type
top
(resp.
bottom
), we refer e.g. to
[
Via
]
. Thus,
\pi^{\prime}
can be determined from
\pi
and
\tau
only (independently on
\lambda
) and

\lambda^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
are proportional to
B^{*}_{\gamma}\lambda
and
B^{*}_{\gamma}\tau
respectively, where
B_{\gamma}
is an elementary matrix which is fully determined by
\pi
and
\tau
.

7.3.2.
Markovian structure

One can furthermore show that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
has a
Markovian structure
in the following sense. For any
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
, if we denote by
\Delta_{\pi}^{t}
(resp.
\Delta_{\pi}^{b}
) the set of
\lambda\in\Delta_{d}
such that
(\pi,\lambda)
is of type top (resp. bottom), and by
\Theta_{\pi}^{t}
(resp.
\Theta_{\pi}^{b}
) the set of
\tau\in\Theta_{\pi}
such that (
39
) holds, then, if
\pi_{0}
(resp.
\pi_{1}
) is obtained from
\pi
performing a top (resp. bottom) operation, then

(40)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}\left(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{\pi}^{t}\times\Theta_{\pi}\right)=\{\pi_{0}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta^{t}_{\pi_{0}}

\displaystyle\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}\left(\{\pi_{0}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta^{t}_{\pi_{0}}\right)=\{\pi\}\times\Delta^{t}_{\pi}\times\Theta_{\pi},

(41)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}\left(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{\pi}^{b}\times\Theta_{\pi}\right)=\{\pi_{1}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta^{b}_{\pi_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}\left(\{\pi_{1}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta^{b}_{\pi_{1}}\right)=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{\pi}^{b}\times\Theta_{\pi}.

Let us recall that
p:\widehat{X}\to X
denotes the projection
p(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=(\pi,\lambda)
. Let
p^{\prime}
denote the projection on the
\tau
-coordinate, namely

(42)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}:\widehat{X}(\mathcal{R})\to\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}\{\pi\}\times\Theta_{\pi},

\displaystyle(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\mapsto p^{\prime}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\doteqdot(\pi,\tau).

From Remark
7.1
,
the following two observations about the orbits of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
(where (F) stands for
future
, (P) for
past
iterates) follow and describe the local product structure.
37
37

37

We remark that these properties show that the invertible map
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
is a
fibred system
in the sense of Schweiger, see

[
Sc95
]
.

Assume for simplicity that
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
and
({\pi},\overline{\lambda},\overline{\tau})
are two triples in
\widehat{X}
with the same combinatorial datum
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
, for which the orbit
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}(T)
is defined for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(F)

If
\lambda=\overline{\lambda}
, then for every
n>0
, the
future
orbits of
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
and
(\pi,{\lambda},\overline{\tau})
both lift the orbit
\mathcal{Z}^{n}(\pi,\lambda)
of the IET
(\pi,\lambda)
, i.e. they both belong to the same
p
-fiber:

p\,(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}\left(\pi,\lambda,\tau\right))=p\,\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}\left(\overline{\pi},\overline{\lambda},\overline{\tau}\right)=(\pi^{(n)},{\lambda}^{(n)})=\mathcal{Z}^{n}(\pi,\lambda).

(P)

If
\tau=\overline{\tau}
, then for every
n<0
, the
past
orbits of
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
and
(\pi,\overline{\lambda},{\tau})
both belong to the same
p^{\prime}
-fiber:

p^{\prime}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}\left(\pi,\lambda,\tau\right))=p^{\prime}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}\left(\overline{\pi},\overline{\lambda},\overline{\tau}\right)=(\pi^{(n)},{\tau}^{(n)}).

Let us mention that it is possible (although we do not want to do it here) to define a map
\mathcal{Z}^{\ast}
on the space
\cup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}(\{\pi\}\times\Theta_{\pi})
such that

\pi^{\prime}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\right)=\mathcal{Z}^{\ast}(\pi,\tau),\qquad\text{which gives}\qquad p^{\prime}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-n}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\right)=(\mathcal{Z}^{\ast})^{n}(\pi,\tau),

so that (P) can have a similar form to
(F)
. The map
\mathcal{Z}^{\ast}
is then
dual
to Zorich induction
38
38

38

The curious reader may be interested in the paper
[
InNa
]
by Inoue and Nakada, where it is shown that the dual of Rauzy-Veech induction can be realized by an algorithm known in the literature as
da Rocha induction
.
in the sense of Schweiger (see

[
Sc95
]
).

7.3.3.
Two-sided cylinders and Markov shifts

Let
\gamma
be a path on
\mathcal{R}
starting from
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
. The simplex
\Delta_{\gamma}
defined in §
7.1.4
by (
37
) consists of all length parameters
\lambda
of all triples
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
which share the first (future)
n
steps of the (invertible) Rauzy-Veech induction. Similarly, by the local product structure,
there exists a set
\Theta_{\gamma}\subset\Theta_{\pi^{\prime}}
which consists of all parameters
\tau^{\prime}
of the triples
(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
which share the
past

n
steps of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
(i.e. the first
n
iterates of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}
). This set is a simplicial cone defined by

(43)

\Theta_{\gamma}:=\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}=(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}(\Theta_{\pi})=\left\{(B_{\gamma}^{*})^{-1}\tau\ :\ \tau\in\Theta_{\pi}\right\}\subset\Theta_{\pi^{\prime}}.

The Markovian structure extends to these sets: if
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
is obtained concatenating
n
arrows,
iterating
n
times the Markovian structure of one Rauzy-Veech induction move given by (
40
) and (
41
), we get by induction that:

(44)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n}\left(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times\Theta_{\pi}\right)=\{\pi^{\prime}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}.

The product sets
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{\alpha}\times\Theta_{\beta}
where
\alpha
and
\beta
are finite paths respectively starting and ending at
\pi
play the role of
bi-sided cylinders

for the symbolic coding of the induction, since they consist of all triples in
\widehat{X}
which share the (finitely many) backward and forward steps determined by
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively.

7.4.
Kerckhoff’s lemma and distortion bounds

Finally, we want to recall some of the
distortion
properties of the iterates of the induction, which relate distortion of the matrices which describe the algorithm (given by ratios of norms of columns) to distortion of volumes of cylinder sets for the induction.

7.4.1.
Notation for Rauzy graph paths

Throughout this §
7.4
, we refer mostly to the notation and the results used in
[
AGY
]
, that we now recall.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a Rauzy class. Denote by
\Pi(\mathcal{R})
the set of paths in
\mathcal{R}
and for every
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
let
|\gamma|
be the length of
\gamma
.
For any
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
, let us denote by
\Delta_{\pi}=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}
the copy of the simplex indexed by
\pi
.
Thus we can write the phase space of (non-invertible) Rauzy induction (defined by (
33
) in §
7.1
) as
X(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}\Delta_{\pi}
.

We use the notation introduced in §
7.1.3
to index the products of matrices
B_{\gamma}
which give iterates of
\mathcal{V}
through the
path

\gamma
in the Rauzy-graph which describes the iterate.
If
\gamma
is a path on
\mathcal{R}
obtained by concatenation
\gamma=\alpha\beta
of two paths, set
B_{\gamma}:=B_{\beta}B_{\alpha}
.
For any path
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
starting at
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
we write for short
B^{*}_{\gamma}:\Delta_{\pi^{\prime}}\to\Delta_{\pi}
for the map which is given on the lengths coordinates
by the projectivization of the linear map
B^{*}_{\gamma}
, as in (
34
).

7.4.2.
Columns balance

For any non-negative matrix
M\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
denote (using the notation introduced by Chaika in
[
Cha
]
) by
C_{i}(M)
its
i
-column and by
|C_{i}(M)|
the sum of its entries. Denote by
C_{\max}(M)
and
C_{\min}(M)
the columns for which the sum of entries are maximal or minimal respectively. We say that
M
is
C
-
balanced
for some
C>1
if

\frac{|C_{\max}(M)|}{|C_{\min}(M)|}\leqslant C.

Remark that, if
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is the transpose of one of the matrices
B_{\gamma}
associated to an arrow
\gamma
by a step of Rauzy-Veech induction (see §
7.1.1
), since

B_{\gamma}
has
1
on the diagonal and only one
1
off the diagonal, we have that

(45)

|C_{\max}(M\cdot B^{*}_{\gamma})|\leqslant 2|C_{\max}(M)|.

It follows that

(46)

|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|\leqslant 2^{|\gamma|}\text{ for {every} }\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}).

7.4.3.
Unique ergodicity contraction

For any
(\pi,\lambda)\in X(\mathcal{R})
satisfying Keane’s condition let
\gamma
be the infinite path in
\mathcal{R}
that indicated successively the types of all steps of the Rauzy-Veech induction and for any natural
n
let
\gamma_{[1,n]}
be its prefix of length
n
, i.e.
\gamma_{[1,n]}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{n}
where
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n}
are the first
n
arrows of
\gamma
.
Consider the simplexes
\Delta_{\gamma_{[1,n]}}
, defined as in §
7.1.4
, see (
37
).
Then
\Delta_{\gamma_{[1,n]}}
, for
n\geqslant 1
form a decreasing sequence of sets (simplices) containing
(\pi,\lambda)
.

As shown by Veech in
[
Vee82
]
, for a.e.
(\pi,\lambda)
, we have

(47)

\bigcap_{n\geqslant 1}\Delta_{\gamma_{[1,n]}}=\{(\pi,\lambda)\}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{[1,n]}})|=+\infty.

The above result is actually equivalent to unique ergodicity of almost every
T=(\pi,\lambda)
(since the intersection in (
47
) can be shown to be in one-to-one correspondence with the cone of invariant measures for
T
).

7.4.4.
Volumes of cylinders

Let us denote by
m
the Lebesgue measure on
X(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}\Delta_{\pi}
, which we recall, for every measurable
A\subset\Delta_{d}
and every
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
, is given by

m(\{\pi\}\times A)=d!\,Leb(\{tx:x\in A,\ t\in[0,1]\}).

One can relate the Lebesgue measure of this simplex in terms of columns using the following formula (proved as Proposition 5.4 in
[
Vee78
]
):

m(\Delta_{\gamma})=\frac{1}{|C_{1}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|\cdots|C_{d}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|},

so
m(\Delta_{\pi})=1
for every
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
.
Moreover, by Corollary 1.2 in
[
Ker
]
(see also
[
Vee78
]
for details), if
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is
C
-balanced then

(48)

C^{-d}\frac{m(A_{1})}{m(A_{2})}\leqslant\frac{m(B^{*}_{\gamma}A_{1})}{m(B^{*}_{\gamma}A_{2})}\leqslant C^{d}\frac{m(A_{1})}{m(A_{2})}\quad\text{ for all measurable }\quad A_{1},A_{2}\subset\Delta_{\pi^{\prime}}.

7.4.5.
Kerckhoff’s lemma

We recall now a key estimate which will be used later in §
9
. The statement was first proved by Kerckhoff in
[
Ker
]
and is known as
Kerckhoff’s lemma
. The proof of this estimate is also presented by Avila, Gouëzel, and Yoccoz in
[
AGY
]
. We here state it in the version using the notation in
[
AGY
]
.

Proposition 7.1

(Kerckhoff’s Lemma, Corollary 1.7 in
[
Ker
]
and Theorem A.2 in
[
AGY
]
)
.

For every Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
there exists a constant
C>1
such that for every
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
there exists a finite
39
39

39

The claim that the set of paths is
finite
is not part of the statement of Theorem A.2 in
[
AGY
]
, but since the inequality in (
49
) is sharp, we can restrict to a smaller finite subset of
\Gamma(\gamma)
, without violating all its properties described in Proposition
7.1
. Therefore, the set
\Gamma(\gamma)
can always be chosen to be finite.
subset
\Gamma(\gamma)\subset\Pi(\mathcal{R})
such that for every
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\gamma)
the following properties hold:

(K1)

\gamma
is a prefix of
\tilde{\gamma}
;

(K2)

B^{*}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
is
C
-balanced;

(K3)

|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\tilde{\gamma}})|\leqslant C|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|
.

Moreover,
\Gamma(\gamma)
is prefix-free, that is, no path in
\Gamma(\gamma)
is a prefix of any other path, and

(49)

\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\gamma)}m(\Delta_{\tilde{\gamma}}|\Delta_{\gamma})>C^{-1}.

Kerckhoff’s lemma is the key ingredient in the proofs of exponential mixing of Rauzy-Veech induction and of the Teichmueller geodesic flow (see
[
AGY
]
and
[
AvBu
]
). It is also used by Chaika in the proof in
[
Cha
]
that almost every pair of IETs is disjoint. We will use it in Section
9
.

8.
Rigidity times with trimmed derivative control

In this section we prove Proposition
5.6
, showing the existence of
rigid towers
(as defined by Definition
5.2
) with
trimmed derivative linear bounds
(in the sense of Definition
5.4
in §
5.2.2
). In §
8.1
, we first provide a more precise version of the result on trimmed Birkhoff sums proved by the third author in
[
Ulc11
]
, which provides the desired trimmed Birkhoff sums linear bounds, but along sequences of towers which are
balanced
.

To produce a rigid time which still has the trimmed linear derivative bounds, we will use that linear bounds in
large
towers persist for a finite number of steps of the induction (see §
8.2.1
) and, crucially, that the Markovian structure of Rauzy-Veech induction (see §
7.3.2
) allows to find rigid times finitely many steps after a balanced one (as shown in §
8.2
).
In the final subsection §
8.3
we explain how to combine these results to conclude the proof of Proposition
5.6
.

8.1.
Trimmed derivative bounds using Rauzy-Veech induction

Let us first state a
technical
version of a result proved by the last author in
[
Ulc11
]
, which allows to construct towers
where the linearly bounded trimmed derivative bounds hold (see Definition
5.4
) using suitable return times of Rauzy-Veech induction.

Proposition 8.1

(acceleration for bounded trimmed derivative sums)
.

For every irreducible permutation
\pi
in any Rauzy class
\mathcal{R}
, and every
positive matrix
B=B_{\gamma}
associated to a neat
40
40

40

A
neat
path
\gamma
, according to the definition given in
[
AGY
]
, is a path
\gamma
starting and ending in
\pi
such that
B_{\gamma}
is positive,
(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}(\overline{\Theta}_{\pi}\setminus\{0\})\subset\Theta_{\pi}
and if
\gamma=\gamma_{0}\gamma_{e}=\gamma_{s}\gamma_{0}
, then either
\gamma_{0}
is trivial, or
\gamma_{0}=\gamma
. Assuming that a path is neat is a technical condition which simplifies some proofs by ensuring that each branch of the first return map on
\Delta_{\gamma}
starts with the matrix
B_{\gamma}
.
path
\gamma
in
\mathcal{R}
, there exists a subset
Y\subset\Theta_{\pi}
with closure
\overline{Y}\subsetneq\Theta_{\pi}
, invariant under rescaling, i.e. such that
tY=Y
for all real
t>0
,
and with
Leb(Y)>0
, such that the set

E:=(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times Y)^{{(1)}}=\{(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}^{{(1)}}\,|\,\,\lambda\in\Delta_{B},\,\tau\in Y\}

(where
\Delta_{B}
is defined as in (
36
))
has measure
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}(E)>0
and has the following property:
if

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
is recurrent to
E
under
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence of visits, i.e.

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{(n_{\ell})}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E\quad\textrm{for\ every}\ \ell\in\mathbb{N},

then every
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)

has linearly bounded trimmed derivative Birkhoff sums for the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
along the towers
(\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
over the inducting intervals
I^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
.

For the definition of linearly bounded trimmed Birkhoff sums along the towers, we refer the reader to Proposition
5.5
, see (
15
).

Remark 8.2
.

Notice that since the length vectors
\lambda^{(n_{\ell})}
belong to a precompact
\Delta_{B}
by definition of
E
, the induction times
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
are
balanced
, i.e. the ratios
\lambda^{(n_{\ell})}_{i}/\lambda^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
are all uniformly bounded.

This Proposition is implicitly proved in
[
Ulc11
]
: in Proposition 4.2 of
[
Ulc11
]
the existence of a set
E\subset\widehat{X}^{(1)}
with the above property is proved. The particular form of
E
in Proposition
8.1
, though, namely that
E
has the product structure
E=(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times Y)^{(1)}
for some
Y\subset\Theta_{\pi}
, plays a crucial role in the following arguments. The fact
that
E

can
be chosen to be of this form is implicit in the proof of Proposition 4.2 of
[
Ulc11
]
. We provide some details and more comments in Appendix
C
.
We will use this proposition together with the Markovian loss of memory described in the next subsection to prove the existence of rigid times in Proposition
5.6
.

8.2.
Markovian loss of memory

We now show that the Markovian structure described in §
7.3
allows to find a rigid Rauzy-Veech time finitely many steps after one of the times given by Proposition
8.1
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
B:=B_{\gamma}
be the matrix associated to a path
\gamma
in
\mathcal{R}
of length
|\gamma|=n
, starting at
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
. Given any (open and) non-empty subset
\mathcal{N}\subset\Delta_{d}
, there exists a non-empty subset
\mathcal{U}
of IETs in
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}
, of the form
\mathcal{U}=\{\pi\}\times U
for
U\subset\Delta_{B}
(open and) non-empty such that

T=(\pi,\lambda)\in\mathcal{U}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{V}^{n}(T)\in\{\pi^{\prime}\}\times\mathcal{N}.

Proof.

The statement follows from the Markovian structure described in §
7.3.3
. Indeed, if we denote by
B_{\gamma}=B_{1}\dots B_{n}
the Rauzy-Veech matrix corresponding to the
n
top/bottom moves prescribed by the path
\gamma
and denote by

\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}
and
\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}
the simplices defined in §
7.1.4
and §
7.3.3
respectively,
by the Markovian relation (
44
) for
B:=B_{\gamma}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n}\left(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times\Theta_{\pi}\right)=\{\pi^{\prime}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{B},

i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n}
maps injectively and surjectively
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times\Theta_{\pi}
to
\{\pi^{\prime}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{B}
. Therefore, given any non-empty (open)
\mathcal{N}\subset\Delta_{d}
, the
set
\{\pi^{\prime}\}\times\mathcal{N}\times\Theta_{B}
has a non-empty (open) preimage.
Explicitly, one can check that the preimage has the form

\{\pi\}\times\mathcal{U}\times\Theta_{\pi},\quad\text{where}\ \ \mathcal{U}=\left\{\frac{B^{*}\lambda}{|B^{*}\lambda|}:\lambda\in\mathcal{N}\right\}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

8.2.1.
Persistence of linear bounds

We will need also the following Lemma, that shows that linear bounds on trimmed derivatives persist in large towers obtained by cutting and stacking a finite number of Rohlin towers.

Given a Keane IET, if
\{I^{(n)},\ {n\in\mathbb{N}}\}
is the sequence of inducing intervals given by (an acceleration of) Rauzy-Veech induction, we denote by
(\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{j})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
the Rohlin towers over the continuity intervals
I^{(n)}_{j}
given by

\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{j}:=\left\{I^{(n)}_{j},T(I^{(n)}_{j}),\dots,T^{h^{(n)}_{j}-1}I^{(n)}_{j}\right\},\qquad 1\leqslant j\leqslant d,

where
h^{(n)}_{j}
is the first return time of
I^{(n)}_{j}
to
I^{(n)}
.
Recall Definition
5.4
for the definition of
M
-bounded trimmed derivatives.

Lemma 8.4
.

Given a Keane IET, let
\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{j}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, be Rohlin towers given by Rauzy-Veech induction. Assume that there exists a subsequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

and a constant
M>0
such that

f
has
M
-bounded trimmed derivatives along each of the sequences of towers
(\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
.

Then if
(\mathcal{T}^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of towers of the form
\mathcal{T}^{\ell}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}
, where
j_{\ell}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
(m_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence such that:

(A1)

(finite linear combination)
there exists natural
K
such that, for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

m_{\ell}\geqslant n_{\ell}
and
h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}
can be decomposed into at most
K
towers of level
n_{\ell}
, i.e.

h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}=\sum_{k=0}^{k_{\ell}}h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}},\qquad\text{for\ some}\ \ k_{\ell}<K\quad\text{and}\quad i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{k_{\ell}}\in\{1,\dots,d\};

(A2)

(large tower)

there exists
\delta>0
such that the tower
\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}
has measure

\left|\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}\right|=\lambda^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}\geqslant\delta>0\quad\text{for all}\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N},

then

f
has
M^{\prime}
-bounded trimmed derivatives along
(\mathcal{T}^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
where
M^{\prime}
depends only on
K
and
\delta
coming from

(A1)
and
(A2)
respectively.

The first assumption (A1) implies that the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}{=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}}
is obtained by cutting and stacking at most
K
Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
of the previous level
n_{\ell}
. Notice that this condition is automatically satisfied if
the product of the cocycle matrices
between time
n_{\ell}
and
m_{\ell}
has uniformly bounded norm independently on
\ell
. Moreover, we remark that if the times
(m_{\ell})_{\ell}
of a sequence that satisfies (A1) happens to be all balanced, then
condition (A2) (and hence the
M^{\prime}
-trimmed derivative bound given by the conclusion) holds automatically for any sequence of towers of the form
(\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}})_{\ell}
, i.e. for any choice of sequence
(j_{\ell})_{\ell}
with

j_{\ell}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
.

The rest of this subsection is spent on the proof of Lemma
8.4
.

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

For short, write

J_{\ell}:=I^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}},\qquad h_{\ell}:=h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{j_{\ell}}

for respectively the base, the height and the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
.
Fix any
x
in the base
J_{\ell}
.
Since the towers of level
m_{\ell}
are obtained by cutting and stacking towers of level
n_{\ell}
,
and any
0\leqslant r\leqslant h_{\ell}
,
we can decompose the Birkhoff sum over the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
(which has one point for each floor of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
) into at most
K
Birkhoff sums, each of length at most
h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i}
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
, as follows.

Set
x_{0}:=x
and denote by
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
(
n\leqslant k_{\ell}
) the successive visits of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
to
I^{(n_{\ell})}
.
Let
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}
be the sequence of indexes in
\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that

x_{k}\in I^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}},\quad\text{for}\ 0\leqslant k\leqslant n.

To simplify the notation, let us also write
h^{\ell}_{k}:=h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}}
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
.
Then, we have the decomposition:

(50)

S_{r}f^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}S_{h^{\ell}_{k}}f^{\prime}(x_{k})+S_{r_{n}}f^{\prime}(x_{n}),\quad\text{where}\,0\leqslant r_{n}\leqslant h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{n_{\ell}}}.

To keep the notation compact, set also
h^{\ell}_{n}:=r_{n}
. Then, considering the lengths of the sums (which are heights of towers), we also have that

(51)

r=h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{0}}+h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{1}}+\dots+h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{n-1}}+r_{n}=:h^{\ell}_{0}+h^{\ell}_{1}+\dots+h^{\ell}_{n-1}+h^{\ell}_{n}.

By assumption (A1), since
r\leqslant h_{\ell}
, we have that
the number
n
of terms in the decomposition is bounded by
n<K
.

For each of the Birkhoff sums that appear in the decomposition (
50
), recalling the definition of trimmed Birkhoff sums and related notation (see §
5.1.3
) and using the trimmed Birkhoff sums derivative control for the towers
\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}}
, we have that

\left|S_{h^{\ell}_{k}}f^{\prime}(x_{k})+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{C^{+}_{j}}{m_{j}^{+}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{C^{-}_{j}}{m_{j}^{-}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}\right|=\left|\widetilde{S}_{h^{\ell}_{k}}f^{\prime}(x_{k})\right|\leqslant Mh^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}},

for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
. If now, for every
j
involved, we
exclude
the contributions of the closest points of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
to a singularity
\beta_{j}
from the right and left respectively, namely the terms

\frac{C_{j}^{+}}{{m_{j}^{+}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{{k}})}}\ \text{for}\ 0\leqslant k\leqslant n\ \text{and}\ \frac{C_{j}^{-}}{{m_{j}^{-}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}}\ \text{for}\ 0\leqslant k\leqslant n,

except
the
largest
term of each of the groups of
n+1
contributions, that is achieved for some
0\leqslant k^{\pm}(j)\leqslant n
corresponding to the respective closest visit, so that

\frac{C^{\pm}_{j}}{m_{j}^{\pm}(x_{k^{\pm}_{j}},h^{\ell}_{{k^{\pm}_{j}}})}:=\max\left\{\frac{C_{j}^{\pm}}{{m_{j}^{\pm}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}}\ :\ {0\leqslant k\leqslant n}\right\}.

We claim that the contribution of all the other closest visits can be estimated by
2C_{j}^{\pm}h_{\ell}
for all
\ell
sufficiently large.
Indeed, all the points of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
belong to distinct floors of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
and hence are
|J_{\ell}|
spaced; thus, already the second closest (and hence any other point in the orbit) to each singularity are at least
|J_{\ell}|
far from the respective singularity. Moreover,
|J_{\ell}|\geqslant\delta/h_{\ell}
(since the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
by assumption (A2) has measure
|J_{\ell}|h_{\ell}\geqslant\delta
). In summary, we have

m_{j}^{\pm}(x_{k^{\pm}_{j}}^{k},h_{i_{k^{\pm}_{j}}}^{(n_{\ell})})=m_{j}^{\pm}(x,h_{\ell})\quad\text{and}\quad m_{j}^{\pm}(x_{k}^{k},h_{i_{k}}^{(n_{\ell})})\geqslant\frac{\delta}{h_{\ell}}\ \text{ if }\ k\neq k^{\pm}_{j}.

Using this, and recalling that by definition trimmed of Birkhoff sums

\widetilde{S}_{h_{\ell}}f^{\prime}(x)={S}_{h_{\ell}}f^{\prime}(x)+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{C^{+}_{j}}{m_{j}^{+}(x,r)}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{C^{-}_{j}}{m_{j}^{-}(x,r)},

using this remark and the trimmed Birkhoff sums
we can rewrite the bounds in the decomposition (
50
), denoting
C_{sum}:=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}C^{+}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}C^{-}_{j}
, to get:

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{S}_{h_{\ell}}f^{\prime}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n}|\widetilde{S}_{h^{\ell}_{k}}f^{\prime}(x_{k})|+\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}C^{+}_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant k\leqslant n\\
k\neq k^{+}_{j}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m_{j}^{+}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}C^{-}_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant k\leqslant n\\
k\neq k^{-}_{j}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m_{j}^{-}(x_{k},h^{\ell}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{n}Mh^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}}+n\frac{C_{sum}h_{\ell}}{\delta}\leqslant Mh_{\ell}+\frac{KC_{sum}}{\delta}h_{\ell}\leqslant M^{\prime}h_{\ell},

where in the last inequality we used (
51
) and set
M^{\prime}:=M+KC_{sum}/\delta

(since
n\leqslant K
and
\sum_{k=0}^{n}h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}}\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{k_{\ell}}h^{(n_{\ell})}_{i_{k}}=h_{\ell}
).
This shows that the claimed trimmed Birkhoff sums derivative control holds for
f
along the sequence of towers
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
, thus concluding the proof.
∎

8.3.
Existence of rigid times with trimmed derivatives bounds

In this subsection we prove Proposition
5.6
, thus showing the existence of times where the trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative bounds coexist with rigidity. The idea is to produce rigid times by considering visits to the product set
E
given by Proposition
8.1
and to exploit the memory loss (given by Lemma
8.3
) to concatenate these visits with visit to a set which gives rigidity (simply given by asking that the first tower has large area) which happen after finitely many iterations of Rauzy-Veech induction. Finally, the persistence of the trimmed derivative bounds (given by Lemma
8.4
) is used to show that the bounds still hold at these rigidity times.

Proof of Proposition
5.6
.

We split the proof in steps for clarity.

Step 1 (Construction of the rigidity neighbourhoods)
:
Fix a permutation
\pi
of
d
intervals and
\epsilon>0
.
Consider the following open set
\mathcal{N}_{1}:=\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon)\subset\Delta_{d}
of lengths data

\mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon):=\left\{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})\in\Delta_{d}\ :\ \lambda_{1}>1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right\}.

and notice that if
T=(\pi,\lambda)
has length datum
\lambda\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon)
, then the
displacement

\delta_{1}
of the first (left-most) interval
I_{1}
(given by
\delta_{1}:={T}x-x
for any
x\in I_{1}
)

T
satisfies
\delta_{1}<\epsilon/2
(since it is
at most
the sum of the lengths
\lambda_{2}+\dots+\lambda_{d}=1-\lambda_{1}<\epsilon/2
).

Furthermore, we claim that if
\epsilon<1/2
and an iterate
\mathcal{V}^{n}(T)\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon)
, then
T
has an
\epsilon
-rigid tower (in the sense of Definition
5.3
). Consider indeed the Rohlin tower
\mathcal{T}_{n}=\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{1}
which has as a base
J_{n}
the first continuity interval
I^{(n)}_{1}
of
\mathcal{V}^{n}(T)
and as height
h_{n}
the first return time
h^{(n)}_{1}
of
J_{n}:=I^{(n)}_{1}
to
I^{(n)}
under
T
. Then, since
\mathcal{V}^{n}(T)\in\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon)
, by definition of
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon)
and the above remark on the displacement, we get (since
\epsilon/2<\epsilon(1-\epsilon/2)
since we assumed that
\epsilon<1/2
)

d(T^{h_{n}}x,x)<\frac{\epsilon}{2}|I^{(n)}|\leqslant\epsilon\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)|I^{(n)}|<\epsilon|J_{n}|,\qquad\text{for\ every}\ x\in J_{n},

which shows that
T
has an
\epsilon
-rigid tower in the sense of Definition
5.3
.

Step 2 (Definition of the return sets)
: Let
\Delta_{B}\subset\Delta_{d}

and
Y\subset\Theta_{\pi}
be a simplex and a positive measure set
Y
invariant under rescaling as in
Proposition
8.1
(where
B=B_{\gamma}
for some neat path
\gamma
of length
N
starting and ending at
\pi
)
and consider the corresponding product set
E:={(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times Y)}^{(1)}
.
Choose a sequence
{({\epsilon_{\ell}})}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

of decreasing positive numbers such that
\epsilon_{1}<1/2
and

\epsilon_{\ell}\to 0
for
\ell\to+\infty
and consider the open sets

\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})

defined as in Step 1. By the Markovian loss of memory (see Lemma
8.3
), for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
one can find a non-empty open set
U_{\ell}\subset\Delta_{B}
such that if
T\in\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}
, then

\mathcal{V}^{N}(T)\in\{\pi\}\times\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})
and hence the tower
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{(N)}
is
\epsilon_{\ell}
-rigid.
Similarly, if
\mathcal{V}^{n}(T)\in\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}
for some integer
n\geqslant 0
, then
\mathcal{V}^{n+N}(T)\in\{\pi\}\times\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})
and hence the tower
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{(n+N)}
is
\epsilon_{\ell}
-rigid.
Consider now the sets

(52)

E_{\ell}:=\left(\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}\times Y\right)^{(1)}\subset E,\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

Step 3 (Positive measure of the return sets)
:
We claim that for each
\ell
,
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}(E_{\ell})>0
. Indeed,
U_{\ell}
is an open non-empty set in
\Delta_{d}
and by assumption
Leb(Y)>0
(see Proposition
8.1
), so we know that
\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}\times Y
has positive
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}\times Leb
-measure. Moreover, since

Y
is invariant under rescaling, its intersection with
\bigcup_{t\in[0,1]}Area^{-1}(t)
has also positive
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-measure. By the definition (
38
) of
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}
, this gives
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}(E_{\ell})>0
.

Step 4 (Construction of return times for a typical IET):

Since the invariant measure
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}
for
the Zorich acceleration
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
is finite and ergodic, and by Step 3 the measure with respect to it of any
E_{\ell}
is positive, by ergodicity, for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a set
\widehat{X}_{\ell}^{(1)}
with full measure
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{(1)}(\widehat{X}_{\ell}^{(1)})=1
,
such that for every
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}_{\ell}^{(1)}

there are infinitely many
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{k}(T)\in E_{\ell}
.

If we now consider the intersection
\widehat{X}^{(1)}_{\infty}:=\bigcap_{\ell\geqslant 1}\widehat{X}_{\ell}^{(1)}\subset\widehat{X}^{(1)}
, which has still full measure, for every
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}^{(1)}_{\infty}
we can construct an increasing sequence
(k_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
k_{\ell+1}>k_{\ell}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{k_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{\ell}
(once defined inductively
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{\ell}
, just choose as
k_{\ell+1}
one of the infinitely many
k>k_{\ell}
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{k}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{\ell+1}
).
Since
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
is an acceleration of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
, each iterate of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
is also an iterate of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
. Let us denote by
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
the subsequence of iterates of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
which correspond to the subsequence
(k_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
of iterates of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
, i.e. such that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{k_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
for every
\ell
. Thus, this sequence is such that

(53)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{\ell}:=\left(\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}\times Y\right)^{(1)}\subset E\quad\text{for all}\ \ell\in\mathbb{N}.

Since the Zorich measure
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
on the IET space
X
is given by the pushforward
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}=p_{*}\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure on
X
, it follows that Lebesgue a.e. IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
has a
lift

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
(i.e. a triple
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
in the fiber
p^{-1}(\pi,\lambda)
of the projection map
p:\widehat{X}^{(1)}\to X
) which belongs to
\widehat{X}^{(1)}_{\infty}
. Hence, Lebesgue a.e. IET
T
has a lift for which there exists an increasing sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
that satisfies (
53
).

Step 5 (Shifted return times are rigidity times)
: Let
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
be the sequence of visits of the
\mathcal{V}
-orbit of
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
to
(E_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
built in Step 4.
We now show that the
shifted
sequence

(m_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
of the form
m_{\ell}:=n_{\ell}+N
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e. obtained
shifting
the sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
of return times to
E_{\ell}
constructed as in Step 4 by the length
N
of the path
\gamma
such that
B=B_{\gamma}

gives the desired coexistence times for
T
, independently of the choice of the lift
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
of
(\pi,\lambda)
used to define
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Let us first show that
(m_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

give
rigidity times
for the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
.

Notice first that, by the definition of
E_{\ell}
and (
53
),
the projection
p\circ\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)={\mathcal{V}}^{n_{\ell}}(T)
belongs to
\{\pi\}\times U_{\ell}
. Thus, by the definition of
U_{\ell}
(see Step 2), since
m_{\ell}=n_{\ell}+N
, we have that
{\mathcal{V}}^{m_{\ell}}(T)={\mathcal{V}}^{N}(\mathcal{V}^{n_{\ell}}T)
belongs to
\{\pi\}\times\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})
.
By Step 1, as
{\mathcal{V}}^{m_{\ell}}(T)
belongs to
\{\pi\}\times\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})
, this gives that the Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}
over the first inducing interval
I^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}
are
\epsilon_{\ell}
rigid towers for
T
.
We also claim that their measure
|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|\to 1
as
\ell\to\infty
. This follows since the heights
h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j}
of the Rohlin towers over the inducing interval
I^{(m_{\ell})}

are
balanced
. Indeed, as
h^{(m_{\ell})}=h^{(n_{\ell}+N)}=Bh^{(n_{\ell})}
and
B
is a positive matrix, we have

\frac{h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j}}{h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j^{\prime}}}\leqslant\nu(B):=\max_{1\leqslant i,k,l\leqslant d}\frac{B_{il}}{B_{kl}}.

Since the measure of the base
I^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
is
|I^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}|>(1-\epsilon_{\ell}/2)|I^{(m_{\ell})}|
(by the definition of
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})
), by the choice of
\epsilon_{\ell}\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|

\displaystyle=h^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}|I^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}|=1-\sum_{j=2}^{d}h^{(m_{\ell})}_{j}|I^{(m_{\ell})}_{j}|\geqslant 1-\nu(B)h^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}\sum_{j=2}^{d}|I^{(m_{\ell})}_{j}|

\displaystyle=1-\nu(B)h^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}(1-|I^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}|)\geqslant 1-\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{2}\nu(B)h^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}|I^{(m_{\ell})}|\geqslant 1-\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{2}\nu(B)\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad\ell\to\infty.

Step 6 (Shifted return times satisfy trimmed Birkhoff sums linear bounds)
:
We will now show that for the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
, every
f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives also along the sequence of rigidity towers
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
defined in Step 5. Notice that, by construction,
E^{\ell}\subset E
and therefore
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is by construction a subsequence of the returns for
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
to
E={(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B}\times Y)}^{(1)}
. Thus, by Proposition
8.1
,
f
has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives along any of the (balanced) towers
\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
. On the other hand,
by construction each tower
\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{i}
for fixed
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
(so in particular the first tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}
)
is obtained by cutting and stacking at most
\|B\|
towers
\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
(as one can see since
h^{(m_{\ell})}=h^{(n_{\ell}+N)}=Bh^{(n_{\ell})}
).
Moreover, as we showed in Step 5, the tower
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{\ell})}_{1}
has measure arbitrarily close to
1
as
\ell\to\infty
.
Thus, both assumptions (A1) and (A2) of Lemma
8.4
(giving persistence of trimmed derivative bounds) hold. It follows from Lemma
8.4
that

f\in pure\mathcal{S}\textrm{ym}\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
has linearly bounded trimmed derivatives also along the sequence of towers
(\mathcal{T}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
, which are rigidity towers for
T
with
|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|\to 1
by Step 5. This concludes the proof. ∎

9.
Producing resonant rigid times in given sequences

In this section, we prove Proposition
6.4
, which allows to show that any given sequence
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
contains infinitely many nearly resonant rigidity times. The proof exploits crucially a classical distortion control result for Rauzy-Veech induction proved by Kerckhoff in
[
Ker
]
and known as
Kerckhoff’s Lemma
and ideas used by Chaika in
[
Cha
]
.
We will use throughout this section the notation and preliminaries introduced in §
7.4
.

9.1.
Preliminary constructions

Let us first introduce the type of IETs which we will use to produce (balanced) rigid times.
We first need a result which allows to approximate sets by Rauzy-Veech simplexes.

9.1.1.
Approximation by simplexes

The following technical result shows that we can produce good approximations (in measure) of every closed set by a union of simplexes which are cylinders for Rauzy-Veech induction. The proof will take the rest of this subsection.

Lemma 9.1
.

For every closed subset
F\subset X(\mathcal{R})
of positive measure and every
\delta>0
there exists
n=n_{F,\delta}\geqslant 1
and a subset
\Gamma_{F}\subset\Pi(\mathcal{R})
of paths of length
n
such that

(54)

F\subset\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F}}\Delta_{\gamma}\quad\text{ and }\quad m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F}}\Delta_{\gamma})<(1+\delta)m(F).

Proof.

Let
U:=X(\mathcal{R})\setminus F
. In view of (
47
), for
m
-a.e.
(\pi,\lambda)\in U
there exists
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
such that

(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma(\pi,\lambda)}\subset U.

Choose
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
such that its length
n(\pi,\lambda)=|\gamma(\pi,\lambda)|
is minimal. Then
n:U\to\mathbb{N}
is defined a.e. and measurable. Hence, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(55)

\text{if}\quad U_{\delta}:=\{(\pi,\lambda)\in U:n(\pi,\lambda)\leqslant n\}\quad\text{then}\quad m(U_{\delta})>m(U)-\delta m(F).

Then for every
(\pi,\lambda)\in U_{\delta}
there exists
\gamma_{n}(\pi,\lambda)\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
of length
n
such that
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
is a prefix of
\gamma_{n}(\pi,\lambda)
and

(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma_{n}(\pi,\lambda)}\subset\Delta_{\gamma(\pi,\lambda)}\subset U.

Let
\Gamma_{U}:=\{\gamma_{n}(\pi,\lambda):(\pi,\lambda)\in U_{\delta}\}
. By definition,

U_{\delta}\subset\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{U}}\Delta_{\gamma}\subset U.

Let
\Gamma_{F}
be the complement of
\Gamma_{U}
in the set of paths in
\Pi(\mathcal{R})
of length
n
. Then

F\subset\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F}}\Delta_{\gamma}\subset X(\mathcal{R})\setminus U_{\delta}.

In view of (
55
), it follows that

m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F}}\Delta_{\gamma})\leqslant m(X(\mathcal{R})\setminus U_{\delta})=m(F)+m(U)-m(U_{\delta})<(1+\delta)m(F).

∎

9.1.2.
Selecting distortion controlled simplexes

In this subsection we show that we can select a (finite) subset of paths such that the corresponding simplexes give a good approximation in measure and have good distortion properties given by Kerckhoff’s Lemma. Recall that, by Proposition
7.1
(Kerckhoff’s Lemma), for every
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
there exists a finite subset
\Gamma(\gamma)\subset\Pi(\mathcal{R})
of paths starting from
\gamma
and having good distortion properties.

Lemma 9.2
.

For every
\gamma_{0}\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
,
0<\delta<1
, and every real
r
large enough, there exists a finite collection
\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}=\tilde{\Gamma}_{\gamma_{0},\delta,r}
of finite paths
\gamma
on the Rauzy class
\mathcal{R}
such that:

(i)

all paths in
\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
are prefix-free and have prefix
\gamma_{0}
;

(ii)

for every
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
,
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is
C
-balanced, and
\gamma
has a prefix path
\gamma_{s}
, which is prefixed by
\gamma_{0}
, such that

r\leqslant|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|<2r,\quad\text{and}\quad|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|\leqslant C|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|;

(iii)

and we have,

m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\Delta_{\gamma})>C^{-1}(1-\delta)m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}).

Proof.

Fix any
\gamma_{0}\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
and
0<\delta<1
. Take any
r\geqslant|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{0}})|
. By (
47
), for
m
-a.e.
(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}
there exists
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
such that

(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma(\pi,\lambda)},\quad|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma(\pi,\lambda)})|\geqslant r\quad\text{and}\quad|\gamma(\pi,\lambda)|\quad\text{is minimal.}

Then
\gamma_{0}
is a prefix of
\gamma(\pi,\lambda)
. In view of (
45
), we have

(56)

r\leqslant|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma(\pi,\lambda)})|<2r.

Moreover, by (
46
), we have

(57)

\log_{2}r\leqslant|\gamma(\pi,\lambda)|.

Let us hence define:

\Gamma^{\prime}_{r}:=\{\gamma(\pi,\lambda):\text{for all $(\pi,\lambda)\in\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}$ for which $\gamma(\pi,\lambda)$ is well defined}\}.

Then
\Gamma^{\prime}_{r}
is prefix-free,

\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{r}}\Delta_{\gamma}\subset\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{r}}m(\Delta_{\gamma})=m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}).

Finally choose a finite subset
\Gamma_{r}\subset\Gamma^{\prime}_{r}
such that

(58)

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{r}}m(\Delta_{\gamma})\geqslant(1-\delta)m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}).

Let

\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}=\tilde{\Gamma}_{\gamma_{0},\delta,r}:=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{r}}\Gamma(\gamma).

Recall that, each
\Gamma(\gamma)\subset\Pi(\mathcal{R})
is a finite prefix-free set of paths starting with
\gamma
, given by Proposition
7.1
.
As
\Gamma_{r}
and
\Gamma(\gamma)
are prefix-free and finite, the set
\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
is also prefix-free and finite. Moreover, for every
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
we can find unique paths
\gamma_{s}
and
\gamma_{e}
such that
\gamma=\gamma_{s}\gamma_{e}
with
\gamma_{s}\in\Gamma_{r}
and
\gamma=\gamma_{s}\gamma_{e}\in\Gamma(\gamma_{s})
. As
\gamma_{s}\in\Gamma_{r}
, by (
56
), we have
r\leqslant|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|<2r
. As
\gamma=\gamma_{s}\gamma_{e}\in\Gamma(\gamma_{s})
,
by (K2) and (K3) in Proposition
7.1
, we have that
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is
C
-balanced and
|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|\leqslant C|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|
.
In summary, then
(i)
and
(ii)
hold. We are left to prove
(iii)
. For this,
note that, by (
49
) and (
58
), we have

(59)

m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\Delta_{\gamma})=\sum_{\gamma_{s}\in\Gamma_{r}}m(\Delta_{\gamma_{s}}){\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\gamma_{s})}m(\Delta_{\gamma}|\Delta_{\gamma_{s}})}>C^{-1}(1-\delta)m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}).

This proves
(iii)
and thus concludes the proof.
∎

9.1.3.
Constructing balanced rigid times

For every
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
and
0<\epsilon<1
let

F_{k}=F_{k}(\epsilon):=\mathcal{R}\times\{\lambda\in\Delta_{d}:\lambda_{k}>1-\epsilon/3\}.

Then
m(F_{k}(\epsilon))=m(F_{1}(\epsilon))\asymp\epsilon^{d-1}
. For every
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
let

1\leqslant k(\gamma)\leqslant d\quad\text{such that}\quad C_{k(\gamma)}(B^{*}_{\gamma})=C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma}).

In view of the approximation Lemma
9.1
, applied to the closure of
F_{k}(\epsilon)
, there exists natural
n_{\epsilon,\delta}
and for every
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
there exists a subset

\Gamma_{F_{k}(\epsilon)}
of paths of length
n_{\epsilon,\delta}
such that

(60)

F_{k}(\epsilon)\subset\overline{F_{k}(\epsilon)}\subset\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F_{k}(\epsilon)}}\Delta_{\gamma}\quad\text{ and }\quad m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{F_{k}(\epsilon)}}\Delta_{\gamma})<(1+\delta)m(\overline{F_{k}(\epsilon)})=(1+\delta)m(F_{k}(\epsilon)).

Let us consider the following set

(61)

A_{r}=A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon):=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon).

As for every
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
the path
\gamma_{0}
is a prefix of
\gamma
, we have
A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon)\subset\Delta_{\gamma_{0}}
. We now want to show that IETs in this set have rigidity times in a controlled range:

Lemma 9.3

(simplex for balanced rigidity)
.

For every
0<\varepsilon<1
, the set
A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon)
defined above has the following properties:

(1)

if
T=(\pi,\lambda)\in A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon)
then there exists an
\epsilon
-rigidity time
q
for
T
with
r\leqslant q\leqslant 2Cr
;

(2)

the measure
m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon))
satisfies:

m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon))\geqslant C^{-d-1}{(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}}(1-\delta)m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}})m(F_{1}(\epsilon)).

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
.
Let first prove part
(1)
, namely that the IET has a rigidity time in
[r,2Cr]
.
If
(\pi,\lambda)\in B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon)
for some
\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}
of length
n
, then
(\pi^{(n)},\lambda^{(n)})=\mathcal{V}^{n}(\pi,\lambda)
is such that
\lambda^{(n)}_{k(\gamma)}>1-\epsilon/3
. Let
q:=|C_{k(\gamma)}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|=|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|
. Then the IET
(\pi,\lambda)
has a tower of intervals with height
q
and whose measure is greater than
1-\epsilon/3
. Therefore,
q
is an
\epsilon
-rigidity time. Moreover,

r\leqslant|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|\leqslant q=|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma})|\leqslant C|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{s}})|<2Cr.

Let us now prove
(2)
, namely the measure bound. As
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is
C
-balanced, in view of (
48
),

m(B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon))\geqslant C^{-d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))m(\Delta_{\gamma}).

It follows that

\displaystyle m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}m(B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon))\geqslant C^{-d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}m(\Delta_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=C^{-d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\Delta_{\gamma}).

In view of (
59
), this proves the bound in
(2)
.∎

9.1.4.
Chung-Erdös inequality

Finally, we recall
a classical probabilistic
estimate, known as Chung-Erdös inequality, which will be used to estimate measures in the final proof.

Lemma 9.4

(Chung-Erdös inequality,
[
ChEr
]
)
.

Let
\{A_{i}\}
be events in a probability space. Then

(62)

\mu(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\geqslant\frac{(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(A_{i}))^{2}}{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mu(A_{i}\cap A_{j})}.

The proof of this classical result can be found in
[
ChEr
]
.

9.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.4

To conclude the proof of Proposition
6.4
(which will be presented in the final §
9.2.2
), we first need to estimate the measure of the intersection of the sets
A_{r}^{\gamma}(\epsilon)
constructed in the previous subsection.

9.2.1.
Quasi-independence of the sets
A_{r}^{\gamma}(\epsilon)

Let us show that the sets
A_{r}^{\gamma}(\epsilon)
, for different values of
r
which are sufficiently far apart, are
quasi-independent
in a sense made precise by the following Lemma
9.5
.
For every
\gamma_{0}\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
,
0<\delta<1
and
r>0
, let us define

(63)

\overline{N}_{r}^{\gamma_{0}}:=\max\{|\gamma|:\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{\gamma_{0},\delta,r}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}:=\min\{|\gamma|:\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{\gamma_{0},\delta,r}\}\geqslant\log_{2}r,

where the last lower estimate on the growth of
\underline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}
follows from (
57
).

Lemma 9.5

(quasi-independence estimate)
.

Suppose that
|C_{\max}(B^{*}_{\gamma_{0}})|\leqslant r<r^{\prime}
are such that
\underline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r^{\prime}}>\overline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}+n_{\epsilon,\delta}
, where
n_{\epsilon,\delta}
is the common length of paths in the sets
\Gamma_{F_{k}(\epsilon)}
,
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
. Then

(64)

m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon)\cap A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r^{\prime}}(\epsilon))\leqslant\widehat{C}\frac{m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}(\epsilon))m(A^{\gamma_{0}}_{r^{\prime}}(\epsilon))}{m(\Delta_{\gamma_{0}})},\quad\text{where}\quad\widehat{C}:=C^{4d+1}\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}.

Proof.

By the definition of
A_{r}
(see (
61
)) and (
60
), we have

\displaystyle m(A_{r}\cap A_{r^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r^{\prime}}}m(B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}\cap B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r^{\prime}}}m(B^{*}_{\gamma}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\cap B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})}).

As
B^{*}_{\gamma}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}=\Delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
and
|\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant\overline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r}+n_{\epsilon,\delta}<\underline{N}^{\gamma_{0}}_{r^{\prime}}\leqslant|\gamma^{\prime}|
,
if
m(B^{*}_{\gamma}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\cap B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})})>0
then
\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a prefix of
\gamma^{\prime}
and
B^{*}_{\gamma}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\cap B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})}=B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})}
. Hence,

\displaystyle m(A_{r}\cap A_{r^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r^{\prime}}\\
\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}\text{ prefixes }\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}m(B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})}).

As
B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
is
C
-balanced, in view of (
48
),

m(B^{*}_{\gamma^{\prime}}F_{k(\gamma^{\prime})}(\epsilon))\leqslant C^{d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))m(\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime}}).

It follows that

\displaystyle m(A_{r}\cap A_{r^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r^{\prime}}\\
\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}\text{ prefixes }\gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}m(\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}m(\Delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))\sum_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}m(B^{*}_{\gamma}\bigcup_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}).

As
B^{*}_{\gamma}
is
C
-balanced, in view of (
48
) and (
60
),

\displaystyle m(B^{*}_{\gamma}\bigcup_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{d}m(\bigcup_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{F_{k(\gamma)}}}\Delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}})m(\Delta_{\gamma})

\displaystyle<C^{d}(1+\delta)m(F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon))m(\Delta_{\gamma})\leqslant C^{2d}(1+\delta)m(B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon)).

It follows that

\displaystyle m(A_{r}\cap A_{r^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\delta)C^{3d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))m(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\tilde{\Gamma}_{r}}B^{*}_{\gamma}F_{k(\gamma)}(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)C^{3d}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-1}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))m(A_{r}).

In view of the measure bound given by part
(2)
of Lemma
9.3
, this gives (
64
).
∎

9.2.2.
Final arguments

We now have all the ingredients needed for the proof of Proposition
6.4
.

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

We will prove that the set
\Omega=\Omega(\underline{s},\epsilon)
has full measure by
localizing
it and estimating its local measure using partitions made by the sets
A^{\gamma}_{r}(\epsilon)
built in the previous subsection.

Step 1: Localizing
\Omega
.

For every path
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
,
denote by
\Omega|\Delta_{\gamma}:=\Omega\cap\Delta_{\gamma}
the restriction of
\Omega
to
\Delta_{\gamma}
. We claim that

(65)

\Omega|\Delta_{\gamma}\supset\bigcap_{m\geqslant 1}\bigcup_{n\geqslant m}A^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}(\epsilon).

To see this inclusion, recall that by Lemma
9.3
, if the IET
T=:(\pi,\lambda)\in A^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}(\epsilon)
then
T
has an
\epsilon
-rigidity time in
[s_{n},2Cs_{n}]
. Thus, if
(\pi,\lambda)
belongs to the RHS of (
65
), for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist infinitely many
n
such that
T
has an
\epsilon
-rigidity time in
[s_{n},2Cs_{n}]
. In particular, we can build a sequence
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
where, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
q_{j}
is an
\epsilon
-rigidity time,
q_{j}>q_{j-1}
and
n_{j}
is chosen so that
q_{j}\in[s_{n_{j}},2Cs_{n_{j}}]
. Recalling the definition of
\Omega(\underline{s},{\epsilon})
, this precisely shows that (
\Omega 1
) and (
\Omega 2
) hold and hence that
T
is in
\Omega
. Moreover, from the definition of the set
A^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}(\epsilon)
, each
A^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}(\epsilon)\subset\Delta_{\gamma}
(see §
9.1.3
). Thus,
T
belongs to
\Omega\cap\Delta_{\gamma}
, i.e. to the LHS of (
65
), as claimed.

Step 2. Estimating the local measure.

We claim that

(66)

m(\Omega|\Delta_{\gamma})\geqslant\widehat{C}^{-1}\quad\text{for {every}}\quad\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}).

The rest of this Step will be taken by the proof, which uses quasi-independence and Chung-Erdös inequality.
Passing to a subsequence in
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, if necessary, we can assume that the quantities
\overline{N}^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}
and
\underline{N}^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}
defined as in (
63
) satisfy

\underline{N}^{\gamma}_{s_{n+1}}>\overline{N}^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}+n_{\epsilon,\delta}\quad\text{ for {all}}\quad n\geqslant 1.

Then, in view of the quasi-independence given by Lemma
9.5
under this assumption, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))\right)^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon)\cap A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))+\frac{2\widehat{C}}{m(\Delta_{\gamma})}\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{m(\Delta_{\gamma})}{\widehat{C}}\frac{1}{1+\frac{m(\Delta_{\gamma})}{\widehat{C}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))}{2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}}.

Moreover, by the measure bound given by part
(2)
of Lemma
9.3
, we have

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))}{2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}\leqslant\frac{nm(\Delta_{\gamma})}{n(n-1)C^{-2d-2}(\#\mathcal{R})^{-2}(1-\delta)^{2}m(\Delta_{\gamma})^{2}m(F_{1}(\epsilon))^{2}}\to 0

as
n\to+\infty
. Hence,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon))\right)^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}m(A^{\gamma}_{s_{i}}(\epsilon)\cap A^{\gamma}_{s_{j}}(\epsilon))}\geqslant\frac{m(\Delta_{\gamma})}{\widehat{C}}.

Using Chung-Erdös inequality (see (
62
)),
this gives

m\Big(\bigcap_{m\geqslant 1}\bigcup_{n\geqslant m}A^{\gamma}_{s_{n}}(\epsilon)\Big)\geqslant\frac{m(\Delta_{\gamma})}{\widehat{C}}.

In view of Step 1 this yields (
66
) as claimed.

Step 3: Final arguments.

Let us consider a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
of sub-
\sigma
-algebras of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
of Lebesgue-measurable subsets of
X(\mathcal{R})
so that
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is generated by
\Delta_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathcal{R})
of length
n
. In view of (
47
),
\mathcal{F}
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
.
Therefore, by Lévy’s zero-one law, we have that the conditional expectations

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\to\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}|\mathcal{F})=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}\quad\text{$m$-a.e.\ as $n\to\infty$.}

Since, by Step 2, we have that
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\geqslant\widehat{C}^{-1}
for all
n\geqslant 1
, it then follows from the above convergence that

\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}|\mathcal{F})\geqslant\frac{1}{\widehat{C}}>0\qquad m\text{-almost-everywhere}.

Therefore (since
1_{\Omega}
takes only
0
or
1
as values) we conclude that
m
-almost everywhere
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}\equiv 1
and hence
m(\Omega)=m(X(\mathcal{R}))
, i.e.
\Omega\subset X(\mathcal{R})
has full
m
-measure.
∎

Appendix A
Deviations of ergodic averages

In this Appendix we show how Proposition
6.8
can be deduced from the recent work of the first and last authors
[
FrUl24
]
.
Let us first summarize in §
A.1

some of the results from
[
FrUl24
]
, from which we can deduce in §
A.2
the proof of Proposition
6.8
.

A.1.
Results on corrections of logarithmic cocycles.

We work with the full measure set of IETs which satisfy the Diophantine-like condition defined in
[
FrUl24
, §3.2]
and called UDC (for
Uniform Diophantine Condition
). By
[
FrUl24
, Theorem 3.8]
, almost every IET satisfies this UDC condition.

Associated with each IET
T
satisfying the UDC is a descending sequence of intervals
(I^{(k)})_{k\geqslant 1}
determining the corresponding renormalizations of
T
. For every
k\geqslant 1
and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}og(T)
let
S(k)\varphi:I^{(k)}\to\mathbb{R}
be the induced cocycle defined by

S(k)\varphi(x)=S_{h^{(k)}_{j}}\varphi(x)\quad\text{for {all}}\quad x\in I_{j}^{(k)},\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant d,

where
h_{j}^{(k)}
is the first return time of
I_{j}^{(k)}
to
I^{(k)}
. For every
T\in X(\mathcal{R})
satisfying the UDC there exist piecewise constant (constant on exchanged intervals) maps
h_{1},\ldots,h_{g}
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\|S(k)h_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{(k)})}}{k}={\lambda_{i}}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant g,

where
0<\lambda_{g}<\ldots<\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}
are the positive Lyapunov exponents related to
X(\mathcal{R})
. Standard arguments show that the means of
h_{2},\ldots,h_{g}
are zero and the mean of
h_{1}
is non-zero.

In view of Theorem 6.1, Corollary 7.11 and (7.12) (all) in
[
FrUl24
]
, we have the following:

Proposition A.1
.

For every IET
T
satisfying the UDC there exist bounded functionals
d_{i}:\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant{g}
such that
d_{j}(h_{i})=\delta_{ij}
for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant g
and for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
, we have

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\frac{1}{|I^{(k)}|}\|S(k)(\varphi-\sum_{i=1}^{g}d_{i}(\varphi)h_{i})\|_{L^{1}(I^{(k)})}}{k}\leqslant 0.

If
d_{i}(\varphi)=0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant g
, then
\int_{I}\varphi(x)dx=0
.

Proposition A.2

(Proposition 7.10 in
[
FrUl24
]
)
.

Suppose that the IET
T:I\to I
satisfies the UDC and the roof function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is in
\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
. Let
\xi:I^{f}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable bounded map such that

\varphi_{\xi}\in\mathcal{L}og(T)
and

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\frac{1}{|I^{(k)}|}\|S(k)\varphi_{\xi}\|_{L^{1}(I^{(k)})}}{k}\leqslant 0.

Then
for a.e.
(x,r)\in I^{f}
, we have

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,r))ds|}{\log t}\leqslant 0.

For every
1\leqslant i\leqslant g
, choose a bounded measurable function
\xi_{i}:I^{f}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, we have
\varphi_{\xi_{i}}=h_{i}
and there exists
K>0
for which
\xi_{i}(x,r)=0
for
r\geqslant K
.

Proposition A.3

(Proposition 7.12 in
[
FrUl24
]
)
.

Suppose that the IET
T:I\to I
satisfies the UDC and the roof function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is in
\mathcal{L}\textrm{og}\left(T\right)
.
Then

(67)

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log\|\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{i}\circ T^{f}_{s}\,ds\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\log t}=\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{1}}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant g.

A.2.
Deduction of Proposition
6.8

We now have all the elements for the proof.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

By assumption,
\int_{I}\varphi_{\xi}(x)dx=\int_{I^{f}}\xi(x,r)dxdr=0
. By definition,

d_{j}(\varphi_{\xi}-\sum_{i=1}^{g}d_{i}(\varphi_{\xi})h_{i})=0\quad\text{for all}\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant g.

In view of the second part of Proposition
A.1
, the mean of
\varphi_{\xi}-\sum_{i=1}^{g}d_{i}(\varphi_{\xi})h_{i}
is zero. As the mean of
h_{2},\ldots,h_{g}
and
\varphi_{\xi}
are zero, it follows that the mean of
d_{1}(\varphi_{\xi})h_{1}
is also zero, so
d_{1}(\varphi_{\xi})=0
. In view of Proposition
A.1
, this gives

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\frac{1}{|I^{(k)}|}\|S(k)(\varphi_{\xi-\sum_{i=2}^{g}d_{i}(\varphi)\xi_{i}})\|_{L^{1}(I^{(k)})}}{k}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\frac{1}{|I^{(k)}|}\|S(k)(\varphi_{\xi}-\sum_{i=2}^{g}d_{i}(\varphi)h_{i})\|_{L^{1}(I^{(k)})}}{k}\leqslant 0.

By Proposition
A.2
,
for a.e.
(x,r)\in I^{f}
, we have

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{\log|\int_{0}^{t}\xi(T^{f}_{s}(x,r))ds-d_{i}(\varphi)\sum_{i=2}^{g}\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{i}(T^{f}_{s}(x,r))ds|}{\log t}\leqslant 0.

In view of (
67
), this shows (
24
).
∎

Appendix B
Roth-type IETs distortion

In this Appendix we briefly recall the definition of Roth-type IET as given by Marmi, Moussa and Yoccoz in
[
MMY05
]
and show that one of the consequences of being Roth-type introduced in
[
MMY05
]
implies the result that we stated as Lemma
6.10
and took as working definition of Roth-type in §
6
.

B.1.
Roth-type IETs

We refer in this section to the notation and definitions in
[
MMY05
]
. Let
T
be an IET with no-connection, i.e. an IET which satisfies the Keane condition. In
[
MMY05
]
the continuity intervals of
I
are denoted by
I_{\alpha},\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is an alphabet of cardinality
d
whose elements are called
names
and the combinatorial datum keeps memory of the
name
of the interval
41
41

41

This different convention plays a crucial role in the study of positivity of the matrices of the Rauzy-Veech cocycle, but we chose to use the simpler convention of labelling the intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{j},\dots,I_{d}
in this paper since the names of intervals play no role in our work.
. We say that a name
wins
(or
loses
) if it is the name of the top (resp. bottom) right-most interval and we perform a top (resp. bottom) Rauzy-induction move. When
T
is Keane, Rauzy-Veech induction never stops and the infinite associated path
\gamma
on the Rauzy-Veech class is
infinite-complete
, i.e. all
names
win and lose infinitely often (as it is shown in §
1.2.3
in
[
MMY05
]
, or also
[
Yoc10
]
). Therefore, one can define an acceleration of Veech and Zorich induction whose steps are maximal steps such that all names but one win
42
42

42

More precisely, the acceleration considered is given by the increasing sequence of times
(n_{d-1}(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
defined inductively so that
n_{d-1}(k+1)
is the largest
n>n_{d-1}(k)
so that no more than
d-1
names are taken by the path
\gamma^{(n)}
for
n_{d-1}(k)\leqslant n\leqslant n_{d-1}(k+1)
.
.
Let us denote by
I^{(k)}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
the subsequence of Rauzy-Veech inducing intervals corresponding to this acceleration.
The authors denote by
\lambda^{(k)}=(\lambda^{(k)}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
the vector of lengths
\lambda^{(k)}_{\alpha}=|I^{(k)}_{\alpha}|
of the continuity intervals
I^{(k)}_{\alpha}
of the IET obtained as first return to
I^{(k)}
.
They denote by
Z(k)
the matrices of the corresponding acceleration (in the sense of §
7.2.3
) of the Rauzy-Veech length cocycle and by
Q(k,l):=Z(k+1)\cdots Z(l)
their product for
k<l
, so that (as stated in § 1.2.3 in
[
MMY05
]
) we have:

(68)

\lambda^{(k)}=Z(k+1)\lambda^{(k+1)},\qquad{\lambda^{(l)}=Q(k,l)\lambda^{(k)}}.

Notice that, by properties of Rauzy-Veech induction, if we define
h^{(k)}=(h^{(k)}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
to be the vector whose entries are the return times
h^{(k)}_{\alpha}
of
I^{(k)}_{\alpha}
to
I^{(k)}
, then (denoting by
A^{*}
the transpose of a matrix
A
) we also have that

(69)

h^{(k+1)}=Z(k+1)^{*}h^{(k)},\qquad h^{(k)}=Q(k,l)^{*}h^{(l)}.

The definition of Roth-type IET involves three conditions, called
(a),(b)
and
(c)
in
[
MMY05
]
. For our purposes, we need only condition
(a)
, which constraints the growth of the matrices
Z(k)
in relation to their product
Q(k)
(which grows exponentially), imposing in particular that
Z(k)
grows subexponentially:

Definition B.1

(see § 1.3.1 in
[
MMY12
]
)
.

An IET satisfies condition
(a)
of the Roth-type condition if for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\|Z(k+1)\|\leqslant C_{\epsilon}\|Q(k)^{*}\|^{\epsilon},\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

B.1.1.
Distortion control for Roth-type IETs

In
[
MMY05
]
it is shown that, from the condition
(a)
in Definition
B.1
,
one can deduce the following control of
distortion
of the entries of the lengths

\lambda^{(k)}=(\lambda^{(k)}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
at the special times given by the induction (see §
B.1
).

Lemma B.2

(Proposition in § 1.3.1 in
[
MMY05
]
)
.

If
T
is Roth type, then for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
C_{\epsilon}
such that for all
k\geqslant 0
,

\max_{\alpha}\lambda^{(k)}_{\alpha}\leqslant C_{\epsilon}\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon}\min_{\alpha}\lambda^{(k)}_{\alpha}.

From this we can also deduce an analogous bound for the distortion of the vectors

h^{(k)}=(h^{(k)}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
of return times (defined in §
B.1
).

Lemma B.3
.

If
T
is Roth type, then for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists

C_{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
k\geqslant 0
,

\max_{\alpha}h^{(k)}_{\alpha}\leqslant C_{\epsilon}\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon}\min_{\alpha}h^{(k)}_{\alpha}.

Proof.

In view of Remark 1 in
[
MMY05
, § 1.3.1]
, there exists
C>0
such that

\min_{\alpha,\beta}Q_{\alpha\beta}(k)\geqslant C^{-1}\|Q(k)\|^{1-\epsilon}.

As
h^{(k)}=Q^{*}(k)h^{(0)}
, we have

\frac{h^{(k)}_{\alpha}}{h^{(k)}_{\beta}}\leqslant\frac{\max_{\gamma}Q_{\gamma\alpha(k)}}{\min_{\gamma}Q_{\gamma\beta}(k)}\leqslant\frac{C\|Q(k)\|}{\|Q(k)\|^{1-\epsilon}}=C\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon},

which gives our claim.
∎

B.1.2.
Distortion control for Roth-type IETs

Let us now show that Lemmas
B.2
and
B.3
together with Definition
B.1
of Roth type we can prove Lemma
6.10
.

Proof of Lemma
6.10
.

Given a Keane IET which is of Roth-type, consider the sequence
(I^{(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of the inducing intervals for the acceleration used in
[
MMY05
]
. Using the notation of this paper, we now denote the continuity subintervals of the
k^{th}
induced map by
I^{(k)}_{j},1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, and
h^{(k)}_{j}
the corresponding return time to
I^{(k)}
(notice that to pass from the intervals
I^{(k)}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and
I^{(k)}_{j},1\leqslant j\leqslant d
one has to
relabel
the intervals, but the order does not matter for maximum and minimum lengths estimates).

Heights distortion.
It follows from the height relation (
69
),
the definition of norm (given for a vector
h=(h_{j})_{j}
by
\|h\|=\sum_{j}|h_{j}|
and for a non-negative matrix
A=(A_{ij})_{ij}
by
\|A\|=\sum_{ij}A_{ij}=\|A^{*}\|
, where
A^{*}
denotes the transpose) and Definition
B.1
of condition
(a)
of Roth-type
that, for every
\epsilon>0
, for some
C_{\epsilon/2}>0
,

(70)

\max_{j}h^{(k+1)}_{j}\ \leqslant\|h^{(k+1)}\|\leqslant\|Z(k+1)^{*}\|\|h^{(k)}\|\leqslant C_{\epsilon/2}\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon/2}d\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}.

Since, in view of the return times transformation relation (
69
), we have that
h^{(k)}=Q(k)h^{(0)}
where
h^{(0)}
is the (column) vector with all entries
1
(which are the trivial return times of subintervals of
I^{(0)}:=[0,1]
to
I^{(0)}
itself), we can estimate
\|Q(k)\|\leqslant d\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}
. In view of Lemma
B.3
, this gives

\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon}\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}\leqslant d(\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j})^{1+\epsilon}\leqslant dC_{\epsilon/4}^{2}\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon(1+\epsilon)/4}(\min_{j}h^{(k)}_{j})^{1+\epsilon}.

It follows that

\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon/2}\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}\leqslant\|Q(k)\|^{\frac{3}{4}\epsilon-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{2}}\max_{j}h^{(k)}_{j}\leqslant dC_{\epsilon/4}^{2}(\min_{j}h^{(k)}_{j})^{1+\epsilon}.

Together with (
70
), this gives the first part of the conclusion of Lemma
6.10
(for any constant
M=M(\epsilon)
such that
M\geqslant dC_{\epsilon/2}C^{2}_{\epsilon/4}
).

Lengths distortion.

The proof of the second part (the lengths distortion bound), is analogous: starting from the relation
\lambda^{(k)}=Z(k+1)\lambda^{(k+1)}

and using Condition (a) in Definition
B.1
, the bound on ratios of lengths given by Lemma
B.2
(for
k+1
and
\epsilon/2
) and the observations that

\min_{j}\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}\|Q(k+1)\|\leqslant 1
and
\|Q(k)\|\leqslant\|Q(k+1)\|
, we get:

\displaystyle\max_{j}\lambda^{(k)}_{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\lambda^{(k)}\|\leqslant\|Z(k+1)\|\|\lambda^{(k+1)}\|\leqslant C_{\epsilon/2}\|Q(k)\|^{\epsilon/2}\,\Big(d\max_{j}\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant dC_{\epsilon}\|Q(k+1)\|^{\epsilon/2}\max_{j}\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}\leqslant dC^{2}_{\epsilon/2}\|Q(k+1)\|^{\epsilon}\,\min_{j}\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}\leqslant dC_{\epsilon/2}^{2}\Big(\min_{j}\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}\Big)^{1-\epsilon},

which gives the desired estimate (enlarging
M=M(\epsilon)
if necessary so that we also have that
M\geqslant dC_{\epsilon/2}^{2}
).
∎

Appendix C
Adaptation of the Rauzy cocycle accelerations

In this Appendix we explain how the statement of Proposition
8.1
follows from the proof of Proposition 4.7 in
[
Ulc11
]
.
The proof of this Proposition in
[
Ulc11
]
is based on repeated applications of two Lemmas (stated in §
C.1
) which produce (via Lusin-type arguments) an inducing set for the (invertible) Rauzy-Veech induction on which one has quantitative control on the (backward) growth of the Rauzy-Veech
heights
cocycle (see definitions in §
C.1.4
and §
C.1.5
below).
The key observation is that Rauzy-Veech induction displays a form of
local product structure
(i.e. the length datum
\lambda
