alone determines the future iterates of the renormalization and of the lengths cocycle which is locally constant in
\tau
, while the height datum
\tau
alone determines the
past
iterates of the renormalization and of the heights cocycle which is locally constant in
\lambda
, see §
7.3.1
). For this reason, one can show that the needed Lemmas (which were stated and proved for general integrable cocycles in
[
Ulc11
]
), in the special case of the Rauzy-Veech height cocycles produce an inducing set which has the desired product structure, see §
C.1.8
and §
C.1.9
in this Appendix.

C.1.
Integrable cocycles controlled growth lemmas

We now state the two general Lemmas about integrable cocycles as they were stated and proved in
[
Ulc11
]
and then used for the Rauzy-Veech height cocycle.
We first recall some terminology.

C.1.1.
Cocycles and integrability

Let
(X,\mu,F)
be a discrete dynamical system, where
(X,\mu)
is a probability space and
F
is a
\mu
-measure preserving map on
X
.
A
cocycle

A:X\rightarrow SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
(
d\times d
invertible matrices) over
(X,\mu,F)
is a measurable map which produces the
deterministic matrix product
A_{F}^{n}(x)=A_{n-1}(x)\cdots A_{1}(x)A_{0}(x)
, where for any
n\geqslant 0
we set
A_{n}(x):=A(F^{n}x)
. This product satisfies
the
cocycle identity

(71)

A_{F}^{m+n}(x)=A_{F}^{m}(F^{n}x)A_{F}^{n}(x),\quad\text{for\ all}\ m,n\geqslant 0\ \text{and}\ x\in X.

We will also deal with the
inverse dual cocycle

(A^{-1})^{*}
, where
M^{*}
denotes the transpose of
M
.

If
F
is invertible, let us extend the definition
A_{n}:=A(F^{n}x)
to every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Consider the cocycle
B:=A\circ F^{-1}
over
F^{-1}
and notice that if, for
n>0
, we define

A^{-n}_{F}(x):=A_{-n}(x)\cdots A_{-1}(x)
, then we have that
B^{n}_{F^{-1}}(x)=A^{-n}_{F}(x)
, since

(72)

B^{n}_{F^{-1}}(x)=B(F^{-n+1}x)\cdots B(F^{-1}x)B(x)=A(F^{-n}(x))\cdots A(F^{-1}x)=A^{-n}_{F}(x).

If
Y\subset X
is a measurable subset with positive measure, the
induced cocycle

A_{Y}
on
Y
is a cocycle over
(Y,\mu_{Y},F_{Y})
, where
F_{Y}
is the induced map of
F
on
Y
and
\mu_{Y}=\mu/\mu(Y)
and
A_{Y}(y)
is defined for all
y\in Y
which return to
Y
and is given by

A_{Y}(y)=A(F^{r_{Y}(y)-1}y)\cdots A\left(Fy\right)A\left(y\right),

where
r_{Y}(y)=\min\{r\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,\,F^{r}y\in Y\}
is the first return time.
The induced cocycle is an acceleration of the original cocycle, i.e. if
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the infinite sequence of return times of some
y\in Y
to
Y
(i.e.
F^{n}y\in Y
iff
n=n_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
n_{k+1}>n_{k}
) then
(A_{Y})_{k}(y)=A_{n_{k+1}-1}(y)\cdots A_{n_{k}+1}(y)A_{n_{k}}(y)
.
We say that
x\in X
is
recurrent
to
Y
if there exists an infinite increasing sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
F^{n_{k}}x\in Y
. Let us extend the definition of the induced cocycle
A_{Y}
to all
x\in X
recurrent to
Y
.
If the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
is increasing and contains all
n\geqslant 0
such that
F^{n}x\in Y
, let us say that
x

recurs to

Y

along

\{n_{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
. In this case, let us set, for any
n\geqslant 0
:

A_{Y}(x):=A_{Y}(y)A^{n_{0}}_{F}(x),\quad\mathrm{where}\,\,y:=F^{n_{0}}x\in Y\quad\text{and}\quad(A_{Y})_{n}(x):=(A_{Y})_{n}(y)\quad\text{for}\quad n\geqslant 1.

If
F
is ergodic,
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
is recurrent to
Y
and hence
A_{Y}
is defined on a full measure set of
X
.

C.1.2.
Controlled growth general cocycles lemmas

Consider the norm
\left\lVert A\right\rVert=\sum_{ij}|A_{ij}|
on matrices. Remark that with this choice
\left\lVert A\right\rVert=\left\lVert A^{*}\right\rVert
.
A cocycle over
(X,\mu,F)
is called
integrable
if
\int_{X}\ln\|A(x)\|\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
.
Let us recall that integrability is the assumption which allows to apply Oseledets Theorem. Recall that if
A
is an integrable cocycle over
(X,\mu,F)
assuming values in
SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
, then
the inverse cocycle
A^{-1}
is also integrable. If
F
is invertible, also the cocycle
B:=A\circ F^{-1}
over
(X,\mu,F^{-1})
is integrable. Moreover, any induced cocycle
A_{Y}
of
A
on a measurable subset
Y\subset X
is integrable.

Let us now state the two Lemmas which were proved and used in
[
Ulc11
]
.
For
m<n
, let us denote by
43
43

43

The reader should remark that here the order of the matrices in the product is the inverse order than the one used in (
71
). This notation is convenient since we will apply it to matrices
Z
where
Z^{-1}
is the Rauzy cocycle.

(73)

A^{(m,n)}(x)=A_{m}(x)A_{m+1}(x)\dots A_{n-1}(x),\qquad\text{where}\ A_{n}(x):=A(F^{n}x)\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Lemma C.1

(Lemma 2.1 in
[
Ulc11
]
)
.

Assume
A^{-1}
is an integrable cocycle over an ergodic and invertible
(X,\mu,F)
. There exists a measurable
E_{1}\subset X
with positive measure
44
44

44

The same proof gives that for each
\epsilon>0
there exists
E_{1}
with
\mu(E_{1})>1-\epsilon
.
and a constant
\overline{C}_{1}>0
such that for all
x\in X
recurrent to
E_{1}
along the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
we have

\frac{\ln\|A^{(n,n_{k})}(x)\|}{n_{k}-n}\leqslant\overline{C}_{1},\qquad\forall\,0\leqslant n<n_{k}.

Lemma C.2

(Lemma 2.2 in
[
Ulc11
]
)
.

Under the same assumptions of Lemma
C.1
, for each
\epsilon>0
there exists a measurable
E_{2}\subset X
with positive measure
and a constant
\overline{C}_{2}>0
such that if
x\in X
is recurrent to
E_{2}
along the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
, we have

(74)

\|A_{n_{k}-n}(x)\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{2}e^{\epsilon n},\qquad\forall\,0\leqslant n\leqslant n_{k}.

In the next section we specialize these Lemmas to the Zorich height and lengths cocycles and show that, with essentially the same proof, in view of the structure of these cocycles, they yield sets with the desired product structure of the inducing set.

C.1.3.
Growth control for Rauzy-Veech lengths and heights cocycles

Two cocycles (dual to each other) associated by the Rauzy-Veech algorithm to the Veech map
\mathcal{V}
and consequently to its Zorich acceleration
\mathcal{Z}
(see §
7
). Let us first recall the definition of the two cocycles (dual to each other), namely the length cocycle (in §
C.1.4
) and the height one (in §
C.1.5
). We use here the notation adopted in
[
Ulc11
]
. We then explain the product structure of the invertible induction (see §
7.3.1
), then state and prove the specific form of the two growth Lemmas in this setting in §
C.1.8
. We refer to §
7
for the notation relative to the invertible Rauzy-Veech induction parameter space.

C.1.4.
Rauzy-Veech lengths cocycles.

For each
T=(\pi,{\lambda})
for which
\mathcal{V}(T)
is defined, we define the matrix
B=B(T)\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
such that
{\lambda}=B^{*}\cdot{\lambda}^{\prime}
, where
{\lambda}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathcal{V}(T)=(\pi^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime}/|{\lambda}^{\prime}|)
. In particular,
|{\lambda}^{\prime}|
is the Lebesgue measure (length) of the inducing interval
I^{\prime}
on which
\mathcal{V}(T)
is defined.

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is an acceleration of
\mathcal{V}
,

Z(T)=B^{*}_{\gamma}:=B^{*}_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots B^{*}_{\gamma_{n}}

is a product of the elementary matrices
B_{\gamma_{i}}
(see in §
7.1.3
) associated to a path
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
on the Rauzy graph
\mathcal{R}
.
The map
Z^{-1}\colon X\rightarrow SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
is a cocycle over
\mathcal{Z}
, that we will call the (Zorich)
lengths cocycle
since it describes how the lengths transform (also known as Zorich acceleration of the
Rauzy-Veech cocycle
).

If
T=(\pi,{\lambda})
satisfies the Keane condition so that its Zorich orbit
(\mathcal{Z}^{n}(T))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is infinite, then we set
\lambda^{(0)}:=\lambda
and denote by

T^{(n)}:=\mathcal{Z}^{n}(T)
the IET obtained at the
n^{th}
step of the Zorich algorithm and write
T^{(n)}=(\pi^{(n)},{\lambda}^{(n)}/|\lambda^{(n)}|)
, where
|{\lambda}^{(n)}|=Leb(I^{(n)})
.
If we define
Z_{n}=Z_{n}(T):=Z(\mathcal{Z}^{n}(T))
iterating the lengths relation, we get that

{\lambda}^{(n)}=(Z^{(n)})^{-1}{\lambda}^{(0)},\qquad\text{where}\ Z^{(n)}=Z^{(n)}(T):=Z_{0}\cdot\ldots\cdot Z_{n-1}.

C.1.5.
Return times and heights cocycle.

Associated to an infinite Zorich orbit
(\mathcal{Z}^{n}(T))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the sequence
((\pi^{(n)},{\lambda}^{(n)}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of IETs obtained inducing
T
on the intervals
(I^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the algorithm also produces a sequence of vectors
({h}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
return times
, or
heights
45
45

45

The vector
h^{(n)}
of return times is often called
height
vector since the action of the initial IET
T=T^{(0)}
can be seen in terms of Rohlin towers over
T^{(n)}=\mathcal{Z}^{n}(T)
and
h^{(n)}_{i}
is in this representation the height of the tower with base
I^{(n)}_{i}
.
: here
h^{(n)}=(h^{(n)}_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the vector whose entry
h^{(n)}_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
gives the return time of any
x\in I^{(n)}_{i}
to
I^{(n)}
.
One can verify that

h^{(n)}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}Z^{(n)}_{ij}
is the norm of the
j
-th column of
Z^{(n)}
. Therefore the
height vectors

{h}^{(n)}
can be obtained by applying the
dual cocycle

Z^{*}
, that we will call (Zorich)
heights cocycle
,
i.e., if
{h}^{(0)}
is the column vector with all entries equal to
1
,

(75)

{h}^{(n)}=(Z^{*})^{n}{h}^{(0)}=(Z^{(n)})^{*}{h}^{(0)}.

Following (
73
), for more general products with
m<n
, we use the notation

(76)

Z^{(m,n)}(x):=Z_{m}(x)\cdot Z_{m+1}(x)\cdot\ldots\cdot Z_{n-1}(x).

We omit
x
from the notation when it is clear from the context and simply write
Z^{(m,n)}=Z_{m}\cdot Z_{m+1}\cdot\ldots\cdot Z_{n-1}
.

C.1.6.
Integrability and extension

Both Zorich cocycles, the Zorich lengths cocycle
Z^{-1}
and the Zorich heights cocycle
Z^{*}
, can be extended to cocycles over
(\widehat{X},{\mu}_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}},\widehat{\mathcal{Z}})
by defining the extended cocycle (for which we will use the same notation
Z^{-1}
and
Z^{*}
) to be constant on the fibers of
p
, namely setting

Z(\pi,\lambda,\tau):=Z(\pi,\lambda)
for all
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}
, so that the extended cocycles
Z^{-1}
and
Z^{*}
over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
are constant on the fibers of
p
.
Both cocycles
Z^{-1}
and
Z^{*}
over the (natural extension of) Zorich acceleration
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
are
integrable
(recall §
C.1.2
for the definition): the integrability of the lengths cocycle was proved in Zorich
[
Zor96
]
.
Moreover, as recalled in the previous §
C.1.2
, this implies that any cocycle induced from them is again integrable.

C.1.7.
Fibered cocycles.

In view of the local product structure of the Zorich map
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
, cocycles which are locally constant on the fibers of the two projections
p(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=(\pi,\lambda)
and
p^{\prime}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=(\pi,\tau)
(introduced in §
7.3.1
) play an important role (since they project to cocycles on
\mathcal{Z}
and the map
\mathcal{Z}^{\ast}
respectively).

Consider a product subset
E\subset\widehat{X}
, of the form

E=(\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times E^{-}),\qquad\text{where}\ \ E^{+}\subset\Delta_{d},\ \ E^{-}\subset\Theta_{\pi}

Let us say that the cocycle
A_{E}:\widehat{X}\to SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
obtained by inducing a cocycle
A
over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
to the subset
E

is
\tau
-constant if it is constant along the fibers
\{\pi\}\times\{\lambda\}\times E^{-}
of the projection
p:E\to X
, i.e.:

(
\tau
-c)

for every
\lambda\in E^{+}
,

A_{E}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=A_{E}(\pi,{\lambda},\overline{\tau})\qquad\text{for\ {all}}\ \tau,\,\overline{\tau}\in E^{-}.

Let us recall that, by definition, the extensions of the Zorich length cocycle
Z^{-1}
and heights cocycle
Z^{*}

as cocycles defined over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
are both constant on the fibers
\{\pi\}\times\{\lambda\}\times\Theta_{\pi}
of
p:\widehat{X}\to X
.

Similarly, let us say that the cocycle
B_{E}:\widehat{X}\to SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
obtained inducing a cocycle
B:\widehat{X}\to SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
is
\lambda
-
constant
if it is constant along the fibers
\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times\{\tau\}
of the projection
p^{\prime}
restricted to
E
, i.e.:

(
\lambda
-c)

for every
\tau\in E^{-}
,

B_{E}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=B_{E}(\pi,\overline{\lambda},{\tau})\qquad\text{for\ {all}}\ \lambda,\,\overline{\lambda}\in E^{+}.

If we consider the cocycles
W^{-1}:=Z^{-1}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}
and
W^{*}:=Z^{*}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}
, the local product structure (and in particular the Markovian structure described by (
40
) and (
41
)) shows that also
W^{-1}
and
W^{*}
are constant on the fibers of the projection
p^{\prime}
.

We will also need the following observation, which we state as a Lemma:

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\widetilde{W}
being
W^{-1}
or
W^{*}
.
If
W_{E}
is
\lambda
-constant as a cocycle over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
for a product set
E=\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times E^{-}

and
E^{\prime}\subset E
is saturated by fibers of
p^{\prime}
, i.e. of the form
E^{\prime}=\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times Y
for some measurable
Y\subset E^{-}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
, then the induced cocycle
W_{E^{\prime}}
over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E^{\prime}}^{-1}
is again
\lambda
-constant.

Proof.

This follows since given any two
\lambda
,
\overline{\lambda}\in\Delta_{d}
,
the visit times to
E^{\prime}\subset E
(which is saturated by
p^{\prime}
-fibers) are equal (i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-n}(\pi,\overline{\lambda},\tau)\in E^{\prime}
iff
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-n}(\pi,{\lambda},\tau)\in E^{\prime}
).
It then follows by definition of
\widetilde{W}_{E^{\prime}}
(and since
\widetilde{W}_{E}
is
\lambda
-constant) that
\widetilde{W}_{E^{\prime}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=\widetilde{W}_{E^{\prime}}(\pi,\overline{\lambda},\tau)
, i.e. that (
\lambda
-c) holds and
W_{E^{\prime}}
is also
\lambda
-constant.
∎

C.1.8.
Backward growth under the local product structure

In view of the local product structure and the locally constant nature of the cocycles we are studying (described in the previous §
7.3.1
and §
C.1.7
), the Lemmas stated in §
C.1.2
can be specialized as follows.

Lemma C.4

(backward controlled growth for products)
.

Let
A
be a cocycle over the induced cocycle
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{X}
to a product set
E=\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times E^{-}\subset\widehat{X}
, where
\pi
is an irreducible permutation. Assume that the cocycle
A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
is
\lambda
-constant and
A,A^{-1}
are both integrable. For every

\epsilon>0
there exists a measurable subset
Y_{1}\subset E^{-}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
, invariant under rescalings and with
Leb(Y_{1})>0
, and a constant
\overline{C}_{1}>0
such that for all
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}
(forward) recurrent under
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}
to the product set

\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times Y_{1}\subset E

along the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
we have

(77)

\frac{\ln\|A^{(n,n_{k})}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\|}{n_{k}-n}\leqslant\overline{C}_{1},\qquad\forall\,0\leqslant n<n_{k}.

(where we use the notation (
76
) for the cocycle matrix products).

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 2.1 in
[
Ulc11
]
(here stated as Lemma
C.1
), with the additional insight coming from the fiber structure of the inverse cocycle described in §
C.1.7
.

Proof.

Consider the cocycle
W:=A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
, which is, by assumption,
\lambda
-constant.
Since the cocycle
A
and hence also
W
is integrable, by Oseledets theorem, (using the notation introduced in §
C.1.1
)
the functions
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\mapsto\ln\|W_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}}^{m}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\|/m

converge a.e. as
m\to+\infty
.
By Egorov’s theorem, there exists a set
E_{1}\subset E
such that
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}(E_{1})>0
and the convergence is uniform, so that
\ln\|W_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}}^{m}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{1}m
for some
\overline{C}_{1}>0
and all
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{1}
and all
m\geqslant 0
.
Notice now that since the cocycle
W
is

\lambda
-constant,
the set
E_{1}
is saturated by fibers of
p^{\prime}
, i.e. it is a product
E_{1}=\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times Y_{1}
for some measurable subset
Y_{1}\subset E^{-}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
. Moreover, it is clearly invariant by rescalings
\tau\mapsto t\tau
,
t>0
(since the cocycle
W
does not change if
\tau
is replaced by
t\tau
for some
t>0
).

If
n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{1}
, we have that
\ln\|W_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}}^{m}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau))\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{1}m

for all
m\geqslant 0
.
Hence, since by (
72
) (using the notation introduced in §
C.1.1
)

(78)

W_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}}^{m}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\right)=A^{-m}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\right)=A_{n_{k}-m}A_{n_{k}-m+1}\cdots A_{n_{k}-1}=A^{(n_{k}-m,n_{k})}.

Thus, we get
\ln\|A^{(n_{k}-m,n_{k})}\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{1}m
for all
m\geqslant 0
, which, changing
indices by
n=n_{k}-m
, gives the desired (
77
) for all
0\leqslant n\leqslant n_{k}
.

To conclude we only have to check that
Leb(Y_{1})>0
. For that, notice that the pushforward measure
p^{\prime}_{\ast}\,\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}
on
\Theta_{\pi}
given by
p^{\prime}_{\ast}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}(U):=\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}\left((p^{\prime})^{-1}U\right)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\Theta_{\pi}
. Thus, since
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}(E)>0
by construction, it follows by Fubini that
p^{\prime}_{\ast}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)}(E)>0
and hence that
Leb(Y_{1})>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.5

(backward subexponential growth for matrices)
.

For
A
and
\pi
as in Lemma
C.4
, for each
\epsilon>0
there exists a measurable
Y_{2}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
with
Leb(Y_{2})>0

and a constant
\overline{C}_{2}>0
such that if
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}
is recurrent under
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}
to
\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times Y_{2}
along the sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have

(79)

\|A_{n_{k}-n}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{2}e^{\epsilon n},\qquad\forall\,0\leqslant n\leqslant n_{k}.

Proof.

Recall that since
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
is ergodic, if
f
is integrable, the functions
\{f\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-m}/(m+1)\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converge to zero for a.e.
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E
and hence, by Egorov theorem, are eventually uniformly less than
\epsilon
on some positive measure set for
m\geqslant\overline{m}
. Since as already remarked in the proof of Lemma
C.4
,

W
is integrable as a cocycle over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
, applying this observation to
f=\ln\|W\|
, we can find a positive measure set
E_{2}\subset E
and
\overline{C}_{2}>0
(in order to bound also
\|W(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-m}(\pi,\lambda,\tau))\|
for
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{2}
and
0\leqslant m\leqslant\overline{m}
) such that if
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{2}
, we have

\left\lVert W(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-m}(\pi,\lambda,\tau))\right\rVert\leqslant\overline{C}_{2}e^{\epsilon(m+1)},\quad\forall\ m\geqslant 0.

When
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E_{2}
, since (recalling the definition of
W=A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}
) we have that

W_{-m}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)))=A_{-m}\circ(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{E}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)))=A_{n_{k}-(m+1)}(\pi,\lambda,\tau),

we get (
79
).
We then reason as in the proof of Lemma
C.4
to conclude that, since
W
is locally constant on the fibers of
p^{\prime}
, the set
E_{2}
has the form
\{\pi\}\times E^{+}\times Y_{2}
for some measurable subset
Y_{2}\subset E^{-}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
and that
Leb(Y_{2})>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

C.1.9.
Adaptation of the proof of the trimmed Birkhoff sums bounds.

Let us now sketch how this specialized form of backward controlled growth Lemmas can be used to deduce Proposition
8.1
.
In the proof of Proposition 4.1 in
[
Ulc11
]
,
we show that Birkhoff sums of the derivative display a certain form of
cancellations
, i.e. the closest visits to the positive and negative parts compensate. The good times
(n_{k})
such that the orbits along a tower are sufficiently well distributed for these cancellations happen, are obtained accelerating and inducing several times to subsets obtained via the two Lemmas 2.2 and 2.3 of
[
Ulc11
]
(reproduced here as Lemma
C.1
and
C.2
). We show that, using the new Lemmas
C.4
and Lemma
C.5
instead, the inducing set has the desired product structure.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

To produce the inducing set, in
[
Ulc11
]
the cocycle
Z^{*}
is first accelerated by considering returns to a set
\mathcal{K}\subset\widehat{X}
which, as remarked in
[
Ulc11
]
, can be chosen of the form
46
46

46

It is important for us in this proof that the compact set has this specific form (that corresponds to a
finite
cylinder in the symbolic coding of Rauzy-Veech induction as a Markov chain) rather than being a general compact set.

\mathcal{K}=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}
, where
B_{\gamma}

is a positive matrix of the Zorich cocycle associated to a neat path
\gamma
(refer to (
36
) and (
43
) for the definitions of
\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}
and
\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}
respectively), so that
B^{*}_{\gamma}=Z^{(N)}
for some integer
N>0
(so
N
gives the number of matrices of the Zorich cocycle that are produced to get
B_{\gamma}
).

The induced cocycle (which gives a balanced acceleration) is called
A
. Then, Lemma 2.1 is used in the proof of Lemma 4.3 of
[
Ulc11
]
, to produce an inducing set
E_{B}\subset\widehat{X}
and a new acceleration
B=A_{E_{B}}
given by returns to
E_{B}
. Then, the new cocycle
B
is accelerated again at the very beginning of Proposition 4.1 of
[
Ulc11
]
, applying this time Lemma 2.2 of
[
Ulc11
]
to get a positive measure subset
E_{C}\subset E_{B}
and a new accelerated cocycle
C=B_{E_{C}}
given by returns to
E_{C}
. The new accelerated cocycle
C
is finally accelerated one more time at the beginning of the proof of Proposition 4.2 of
[
Ulc11
]
, where Lemma 2.2 again gives a positive measure set
E_{D}\subset E_{C}\subset\widehat{X}
so that the sequence of returns to
E_{D}
gives the desired trimmed derivative Birkhoff sums upper bounds.

Let us modify the construction as follows. Consider the height cocycle
Z^{*}
over
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mu}_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}},\widehat{\mathcal{Z}})
, which is integrable and
\tau
-constant by definition. Therefore, the cocycle
W:=Z^{*}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1}
is a cocycle over
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mu}_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}^{(1)},\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{-1})
which is also integrable and, as discussed in §
C.1.7
, is
\lambda
-constant. If we accelerate this cocycle to consider returns to the compact set
\mathcal{K}:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}
, the induced cocycle will not be in general
\lambda
-constant. Therefore,
we consider instead the acceleration which corresponds to visits to

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K})=\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}})=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}B_{\gamma}},

where the above relation holds by the Markovian property (
44
) and the definition of
N
(which gives the number of Zorich matrices produced to get
B_{\gamma}
).

For any
Y_{0}\subset\Theta_{B_{\gamma}B_{\gamma}}
, which is invariant under rescaling and of positive measure let

\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}):=\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{-N}}(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times Y_{0})=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times B_{\gamma}^{*}(Y_{0}).

Let us consider two induced cocycles
A:=Z_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})}
and
\widetilde{A}:=Z_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}))}
.
As
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{-N}}\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times Y_{0}
,
in view of Lemma
C.3
, the cocycle
\widetilde{W}:=\widetilde{A}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}))}^{-1}
over
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}))}^{-1}
is
\lambda
-constant. By Lemma
C.4
or Lemma
C.5
respectively, there exists a subset
Y_{1}\subset Y_{0}
invariant under rescaling and of positive measure such that if
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence of returns to
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{1}))
, then

\|\widetilde{A}^{(n,n_{k})}\|\leqslant e^{\overline{C}_{1}(n_{k}-n)}\quad\text{or}\quad\|\widetilde{A}_{n_{k}-n}\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{2}e^{\epsilon n}\quad\text{resp.}\quad\forall\,0\leqslant n\leqslant n_{k}.

Note that
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is also the sequence of returns for
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})}
to
\mathcal{K}(Y_{1})
. Since
A_{n}=B^{*}_{\gamma}\,\widetilde{A}_{n}\,(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}
and
A^{(n,n_{k})}=B^{*}_{\gamma}\,\widetilde{A}^{(n,n_{k})}\,(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}
, we have

\|{A}^{(n,n_{k})}\|\leqslant\|B^{*}_{\gamma}\|\|(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\|\,e^{\overline{C}_{1}(n_{k}-n)}\quad\text{or}\quad\|{A}_{n_{k}-n}\|\leqslant\|B^{*}_{\gamma}\|\|(B^{*}_{\gamma})^{-1}\|\overline{C}_{2}\,e^{\epsilon n}\quad\text{resp.}

for all
0\leqslant n\leqslant n_{k}

Next, when Lemma
C.1
or, respectively, Lemma
C.2
are used in
[
Ulc11
]
, we replace these generic Lemmas with, respectively, Lemmas
C.4
and Lemma
C.5
. More precisely, first consider the induced cocycles

A:=Z_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\widetilde{A}:=Z_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K})}
with
\mathcal{K}=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K})=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{B_{\gamma}B_{\gamma}}
.
Next, Lemma
C.4
applied to
\widetilde{A}
gives
Y_{B}\subset\Theta_{B_{\gamma}B_{\gamma}}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
and the set
E_{B}:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times B^{*}_{\gamma}(Y_{B})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{B})
such that
\|A^{(n,n_{k})}\|\leqslant e^{\overline{C}_{B}(n_{k}-n)}
for
0\leqslant n<n_{k}
, where
(n_{k})
are return times for
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{K}}
to
\mathcal{K}(Y_{B})=E_{B}
.

Next, consider the induced cocycles

B:=Z_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{B})}
and
\widetilde{B}:=Z_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{B}))}
.
Then Lemma
C.5
applied to
\widetilde{B}
gives
Y_{C}\subset Y_{B}
and the set
E_{C}:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times B^{*}_{\gamma}(Y_{C})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{C})
such that
\|B_{n_{k}-n}\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{C}e^{\epsilon n}
for
0\leqslant n<n_{k}
, where
(n_{k})
are return times for
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{B})}
to
\mathcal{K}(Y_{C})=E_{C}
.

In the last step, consider the induced cocycles

C:=Z_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{C})}
and
\widetilde{C}:=Z_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{N}}(\mathcal{K}(Y_{C}))}
.
Then again Lemma
C.5
applied to
\widetilde{C}
gives
Y_{D}\subset Y_{C}\subset Y_{B}
and the set
E_{D}:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times B^{*}_{\gamma}(Y_{D})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{D})
such that
\|C_{n_{k}-n}\|\leqslant\overline{C}_{D}e^{\epsilon n}
for
0\leqslant n<n_{k}
, where
(n_{k})
are return times for
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{K}(Y_{C})}
to
\mathcal{K}(Y_{D})=E_{D}
.
Finally we set
Y:=B^{*}_{\gamma}(Y_{D})
.

We then claim that the visits to the set
E:=(E_{D})^{(1)}=(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{B_{\gamma}}\times Y)^{(1)}
provide the sequence of times
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
for which the conclusion of Proposition
8.1
, namely the Birkhoff estimate (
15
), holds.
For this sequence, all the arguments of the proofs in
[
Ulc11
]
, namely the effective equidistributions and cancellations arguments, can be applied
verbatim
, without needing any modification, when using these new Lemmas (since the only properties used in the proofs are balance, positivity of the matrices and the backward controlled growth given in
[
Ulc11
]
by Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2, which all hold also for the new accelerations since the backward controlled growth estimate is the same now given by Lemmas
C.4
and Lemma
C.5
). Thus, the Birkhoff estimate for the trimmed derivatives holds along the Rohlin towers of the sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Appendix D
Measurability of pairs of spectrally disjoint flows

In this Appendix, we first discuss (in §
D.1
and §
D.2
) some classical reductions which are used to induce a topology and define the period map on locally Hamiltonian flows. The main results of this Appendix are stated and proved in §
D.3
and are used in the proof of Theorem
3
in §
2.3
to show that the set of
pairs
of spectrally disjoint locally Hamiltonian flows is measurable and to make sense of the notion of
typical
pairwise spectral disjointness (which is the main result of Theorem
3
).

D.1.
Identification with closed one forms and topology

Let
M
be a compact connected orientable surface equipped with a smooth (namely
C^{\infty}
) area form
\omega
of total area one. By Moser’s argument (see
[
Mos
]
),
all area one smooth area forms are
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphic. It follows that any locally Hamiltonian flow on
M
is
C^{\infty}
-conjugate to a locally Hamiltonian flow on
M
preserving the area form
\omega
. Denote by
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
the space of such flows.

Flows in
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)

can be identified with the space of closed
1
-forms on
M
.
Indeed, as explained in §
1.1.1
, any such closed
1
-form
\eta
determines a flow in
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
. Conversely, given a flow
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\in\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
generated by a vector field
X
, letting
\eta:=\imath_{X}\omega=\omega(X,\,\cdot\,)
, one gets that
\eta
is a smooth closed 1-form such that
d\eta=0
, since
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
preserves
\omega
.
Thus, equivalently,
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
can be identified with the space of multivalued Hamiltonian functions on
M
. This identification provides a natural
C^{1}
metric on
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
, whose induced topology is Polish. Denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
the subset of Morse
1
-forms for which the corresponding locally Hamiltonian flow has no saddle loop homologous to zero. Notice that the set
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
is open in
\mathcal{LH}(M,\omega)
and thus inherits a Polish topology.

D.2.
Reduction to a fixed singularity set

Every flow in
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
has
\kappa:=2g-2
Morse saddle fixed points (by the Poincaré-Hopf Theorem). Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a finite subset with
\kappa
elements. In view of
[
MiVi
]
, for any two
\kappa
-element subsets of
M
, there exists a
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism of
M
preserving
\omega
and mapping one subset onto the other. It follows that any flow from
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
is
C^{\infty}
conjugate to a flow in
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
such that its set of fixed points is equal to
\Sigma
. Denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)
the set of such flows, which is in turn a closed subset of the set
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
. Let
n:=2g+\kappa-1=4g-3
. Then
n
is the dimension of the relative
1
-homology space
H_{1}(M,\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
. Let
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})
be a basis of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
.

D.3.
Measurability of periods of spectrally disjoint pairs

Consider the subset
\mathcal{SD}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
of
pairs
of flows
\big((\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big)
such that
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are spectrally disjoint.
The first result of this Appendix is the following:

Proposition D.1
.

The subset
\mathcal{SD}
of

spectrally disjoint pairs

is a
G_{\delta}
subset in the product
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
with the product topology.

Proof.

Denote by
P(\mathbb{R})
the space of all probability Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the
weak topology. As it was shown in
[
BuMa
]
, the set

\mathcal{O}:=\{(\mu,\nu)\in P(\mathbb{R})\times P(\mathbb{R}):\mu\text{ and }\nu\text{ are orthogonal}\}

is a
G_{\delta}
subset of
P(\mathbb{R})\times P(\mathbb{R})
. Take any
f,g\in L^{2}_{0}(M,\omega)
. If
\|f\|_{L^{2}}=\|g\|_{L^{2}}=1
, then the maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\to P(\mathbb{R})\qquad\qquad\text{and}\

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)\to P(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma^{(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{f}\qquad

\displaystyle(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\sigma^{(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{g}

(where
\sigma^{(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{f}
denotes the spectral measure of
f
with respect to the flow
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
)
are continuous when
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
is endowed with the Polish topology introduced in §
D.1
. It follows that for any
f,g\in L^{2}_{0}(M,\omega)
, the
set

\mathcal{O}(f,g):=\{((\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}})\in\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega):(\sigma^{(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{f},\sigma^{(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{g})\in\mathcal{O}\}

is a
G_{\delta}
subset. Let
\{f_{i}:i\geqslant 1\}
be a dense subset in
L^{2}_{0}(M,\omega)
. Then the set

\bigcap_{i,j\geqslant 1}\mathcal{O}(f_{i},f_{j})

is a
G_{\delta}
subset of
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega)
. In view of Lemma 21 in
[
Le96
]
, for any finite Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
and any flow
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, the set

\{f\in L_{0}^{2}(M,\omega):\sigma^{(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}_{f}\perp\mu\}

is closed in
L_{0}^{2}(M,\omega)
. It follows that

\bigcap_{i,j\geqslant 1}\mathcal{O}(f_{i},f_{j})=\mathcal{SD},

which completes the proof.
∎

Consider now the set
\underline{\mathcal{SD}}
of
pairs of periods
such that all corresponding pairs of flows belong to
\mathcal{SD}
(which is used also in the proof of Theorem
3
§
2.3
), namely the set
\underline{\mathcal{SD}}
given by:

\underline{\mathcal{SD}}=\{(\bar{v},\bar{w})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:(Per\times Per)^{-1}(\{(\bar{v},\bar{w})\})\subset\mathcal{SD}\},

where

(80)

Per\times Per:\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

is the product of the period map defined in §
1.1.3
, see (
1
).

The main result of this Appendix (that is used to prove typicality of pairs of spectrally disjoint flows in the proof of Theorem
3
, see §
2.3
) is the following:

Lemma D.2
.

The set
\underline{\mathcal{SD}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is Lebesgue measurable.

Proof.

Let us first show that

\underline{\mathcal{SD}}=((Per\times Per)(\mathcal{SD}^{c}))^{c}.

Indeed, we have that

\displaystyle(\bar{v},\bar{w})\in((Per\times Per)(\mathcal{SD}^{c}))^{c}\Leftrightarrow(\bar{v},\bar{w})\notin(Per\times Per)(\mathcal{SD}^{c})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(Per\times Per)^{-1}(\{(\bar{v},\bar{w})\})\cap\mathcal{SD}^{c}=\emptyset\Leftrightarrow(Per\times Per)^{-1}(\{(\bar{v},\bar{w})\})\subset\mathcal{SD}.

In view of Proposition
D.1
,
\mathcal{SD}^{c}
is a Borel subset in
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)
.
Since the period map
Per
(defined in (
1
)) is continuous, also the product map
Per\times Per
in (
80
)
is continuous.
As
\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)\times\mathcal{U}_{\min}(M,\omega,\Sigma)
is a Polish topological space, the continuous image
(Per\times Per)(\mathcal{SD}^{c})
is an analytic set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By the classical Lusin-Souslin theorem, it is universally measurable, in particular, it is Lebesgue measurable. This gives the Lebesgue measurability of
((Per\times Per)(\mathcal{SD}^{c}))^{c}
as well, which completes the proof.
∎
